The late Sir Wemyss Reid used to tell a story of how he :was badly " left " bY,a young reporter OIY- whom he thought he had scored. The rtiblic were in a state, of great anxiety . regarding the fate of . the miners entombed -through the Hartley Colliery explosion in Northumberland. All depended, from the news- paper point of view, on who could get the news to Newcastle first. . Reid had a pony and -trap, and immediately he got the news he drove off at top speed. But his rival could not get a con- veyance for love or money. He saw a good:, ' train approaching, and was seized with an in- spiration. He got in front of the enp-,ine . and waved his arms, compelling the driver to stop. Then, he clambered on the engine, and by pro- mises of F ,t reward prevailed on the driver let hi. Tit to . e to a branch station, whence a connection ' .. -ild be caught_ to Newcastle. That audacious young man, now a well-known London journalist, beat -Reid by half an hour, and his paper .gained a tremendous "scoop." - It was in the interests of "copy" that twc Manchester journalists about a quarter of a century ago played off a joke on the .public. II . is well remembered even now. They • planted themselves in front of the Royal Infirmary,- au'i suddenly evinced an alarmed interest in the roll .of that building. - They gesticulated. Nobody knew what was th( Matter, and it took a long time for the roultituth to gather the fact that they had been - 1 ipaxed. A 11EP3RTER'S ADVENTURE,

[COPYIZIgIII`.]

AR American newspaper contains this para- graph Editor Johnson, .who recently bought tlio Oxford Bee,' Kansas, has filled every posi- .tion•lrom • 'devil' on a .country -weekly up to imanaging editor of a daily nvwspaper. le has also and .experimce in mining, breaking wild horses, working on the range as a cowboy, 811r- re.Ong railroads, driving I{oalcy .Mountain stage coaches, whacking ~ .15-tills and running a saw 'mill, Re is a dead shot with any kind of -weapon, from an elm club to a thirteen-inch gun. lie, has been rolled und4r a saw lo,;, thrown down a Mountain by a bucking bronclio, and hugged by a grizzy hear. .• In short he knows how Co run• a eountry newopaper."' sometimes think (writes a, descriptive re- porter) that -it is very necessary to be able to (:e all that is described in the foregoing and much more,. if one is to -be recognised as a good re- porter. - There are reporters and reporters—the liol':2!,-,ians'on little eoui,try newspapers and the "specials" who draw big salaries from the great ",dailies." The ranks of the latter, of course,:Are recruited from these of the for- mer, and every budding junior has for the prin- cipal picture -in the chambers of his imagery one depicting himself in the editor's chair, giv- ing five minute interviews to Cabinet Ministers. But any man, whatever the class of newspaper for :which he may be working, who has had a few years' experience, is able to recall interest- ing, unusual and exciting adventures through which he has uassed. in -his search for copy. _ _

He must, of course, he enterprising, an-Li able rtct wisely without .waiting for instructions. It is no use having a University education if you cannot write the report of a police court c:.tse so that it, may be understandett, of the people. "JUMP IN!"

There was once a young man from Cambridge who through influence obtained a situation as reporter on a weekly newspaper. On publish- ing- day a big fire broke out in a town twenty miles away, and this particular young man was Ordered to rush ,6? and 'cover" it. The editor waited impatiently for the telegrams, but they did not arrive., The hours,. passed, and there was no adequate report of the fire in type, while the time for going to press was .becoming peri- lously near. No description from the new re- porter! But a .telegram cathe at last: "Have arrived at fire; telegraph further instructions." ' The editor did: This was his ,wire: "Find out ' where fire is hottest and jump in." Sins of commission are more readily forgiv- able on a newspaper than sins of omission. An- other instance of that is afforded by the example of the American reporter who omitted, to get a report of a train°disaster. He had found a big obstruction on the line and removed it in- stead of sitting on the rails until the train and the Smash came along. He was discharged for lack of enterprise.

A YOUTHFUL INDTSCEETION.

One of my first adventures was the -working up of a "disappearance." A young lady, 3 beautiful .girl who had made some impression on my susceptible twenty-year-old heart, and lived in my district, which was about four miles out of town, one day "disappeared. It fell to my lot to report all the efforts made to find her, and my interest was intense. - In a week the girl returned, and told a tale which beat all the fiction I had ever read to fits. It -was a story of how she had been forced across fields at. the muzzle of a revolver, of how finally she was stunned by a terrific blow, and incarcerated in a building, front which she escaped in four days, wandering in this lonely district mile after mile, until she came to a place she knew. Forthwith I had to commence a search for the building in which the girl had been imprisoned, and at last I came across what seemed to be the. identical place, judging from the girl's des- ii: I found torn. Earment6 such as those the girl had lost. My excitemen. grew, for I thought I had made a discovery which would startle an already over-wrought district. But -when I trod noon a man who was in hiding under some hay I fairly jumped. I pretended I was a detective (I, a 'youth of twenty, a detective) arrested that man, and-7 well,- we will draw a veil - It wasn't the bui7di7,-

f:3 CHILDREN'S CORNER, Conducted by UNCLE CALEB.

NEW YEAR'S GIFTS.

In England Christmas is the time when we make our presents, though of course some are made at New Year also. •I thought my readers might like to know that the custom of making new year gifts is a very old one. It is,.indeed, another of those things we - have borrowed from the Romans. In France they keep up the custom and also call it by its Roman name: Right up to the present day the French word for A New Year gift is derived from the name of the Roman god, Strenia, the patroness of gifts. There was a grove in Rome dedicated to this goddess where it was customary to get fresh twigs to give as presents to friends and relatives on NevrAreaFS—Day.- During the sway of the Emperors, Roman subjects made New Year's gifts to the Sovereign. Augustus received such quantities of these that he had gold and silv—er statutes made of them. Tiberius did away with the usage, because he considered it too troublesome to express thanks for the gifts. Caligula,on the contrary, reintroduced the custom, and even made up for his predecessor's refusal toreceive presents by acquiring those that had been offered to him to be given to himself as arrearages. The custom of making New Year's gifts, notwithstanding attempts to suppress it, was continued after Europe had become Christian. For a time, present- making was transferred to Easter, but later it was again associated with the first day of January. Santa Claus has taken the place of Strenia, at least in England, but France and Scotland still keep to the old Roman festival of New Year.

THE OLDEST NEW YEAR CUSTOM. Perhaps the very oldest New Year Custom which belongs to England is observed each- year at Malmesbury. The story is quite interesting. In 937 the Saxon King Athelstan won a great victory at Brunan- burg over a combined army of Danish vikings, wild Scots,. and Welshmen. The men of Malmesbury did yeomen service on, this occasion and Athelstan was not ungrateful. He. not only gave the. town a charter, but • also presented it With a large piece of land to be held as common, and to this day the commoners keep up the old custom of the- grant. On the first of each January the. commoners -are taken, one by one, by the steward of the manor to their allotments The tenant digs a small bole, and the steward places at the bottom of it a new shilling_ He then strikes the tenant three times on the. back and repeats these words: '1 give this. to thee as freely as King A thelstan gave it to me. and hone - I-2.0010r thou Tv"'

Hats off to our forwards, and may their shadow never grow less!

Sonl&thiug in the nature of a record has been established by the fact that ten tries have been scored by members of the forward rank in two matches. Writing from memory, I don't remember any other .occasion on which our forwards so excelled themselves. The old order of the scoring being performed by the 'backs has verily been changed at Wilderspool,and the new method of the forwards piling up the points is .I.3roving very acceptable in cur time of trouble. So 'far as the back division is corcerned, they have been 'merely spectators The last occasion on which one of the backs crossed an opponent's line for a legitimate rtry was on December 7th, when Brooks scored against Huddersfield. That is a long indictment against Itenwick and his colleagues. They will have to get a " move on" if they don't wish to be put into the shade .altogether.

Warrington can claim, despite an unlucky defeat at Oldham, to have done well during the holiday engage- ments To gathr sia out of a possible eight points is not a mean achievement considering our previous disasters. Saturday's gland win over Leeds was the most meritorious this season, and, by the way, we recordtd our highest score. This happy state of affairs was ditectiy attributable to the excellent work ,of the forwards who routed the big Leeds men right and (-'-eft. We had heard so much of the doughty deeds of 'the Leeds pack, wit so easily worsted Hunslet a few 'days previously that we were inclined 'to be a little 'sceptical about the result. But once the game was 'under weigh those doubts were immediately dispelled ,for Thomas and his merry men counterbalanced the ‘disadvantage of weight,by an overwhelming superiority ia the loose.

We have been accustomed to praising the Yorkshire forwards for their strong rushing and dribbling, but nn Saturday's form the Tykes will have to take a pattern from the brilliant Warrington sextette. In the art of gaining possession from the scrimmage Leeds were quite our equals. When it came to smothering the efforts of the backs they were not in the hunt. Better or more robust tackling has not been seen at Wiiders- pool for some time. Quarter was neither asked for nor given, and Campbell, the Australian who. was making his first appearance here, iviil have lively recollections. On the whole the Colonial did not play a bad game. Re knows the way to the line and one effort in the first half almost brought success. He has one weakness which is so apparent in many of our Colonials—that of getting rid of the ball when in a tight corner. The Leeds back division was a better balaticed and more resourceful quartette than ours, but as stated, their efforts to open out the game were frustrated by the keenness of our tackling. There was only one really class movement during the whole game, and Leeds were the participators. The ball was passed with delightful freedom by Haycox, Ileaton,Campbell and Davies. The latter put the finishing touch to a brilliant move by scoring at the corner. The scorer showed a clean pair of heels to the Warrington defenders and the manner in which he swerved past Daintith and Julley earned the admira- tion of the crowd.

Leeds seem to be particularly fortunate in the choice of fall-back. Warrington supporters and players have reason to remember Young, who was chiefly responsible for our exit in the semi-final of the Cup a few seasons ago. In Haycox they have an excellent substitute, NT models his play on the lines of Young. But for Ms brainy and daring play Leeds would have suffered a heavier defeat. He extricated his side from many a dangerous position. One naturally expects something scintidating from Ganley, but for once in a way he was subdued. Whether this was due to the lack of support from his partner Sanders, or the "spotting " of Daihtith is a matter in doubt. Certainly he gave us nothing to enthuse about. Sanders did not relish stopping the bustling dashes of the Warrington forwards. The tall auburn-haired Ileaton, with his lcng aking stride coupled with height and weight, made him a dangerous man when near the line.

Allusion has already been made to the sterling work of the forwards. In taking stock of the back division one cannot overlook the fact that there was an improvement in our half-back play. Nicholas and Daintith worked well together although there was, a •disposition to boot the ball t-ro frequently. Williams ;has yet to break his duck as a scorer. Opportunities were few and far between. On the defence he ,emphasized his usefulness. The committee are beginning to feel proud of their local, recrtiit Davies. He bids fair to !make a dangerous wing. Of all the players who have been tried in that posit on he gives the most promise. He lacks nothing in pluck, and is not afraid of hard work.

The New Year was ushered in with a splendid victory.over our near and dear neighbours from across the river. Local rivalry does not as a rule tend towards good football, but this was net the case on Wednesday, the game being contested in an admirable spirit, Warringt,n winning a hard contest in hand- some fashion. Having started the year in such an auspicious manner, it is to be hoped the victory will be a welcome precursor to a series. Our position in the League calls for an improvement. We have been so accustomed to see Warrington .qtruggling in the last half d. zen that it came as a pleasant surprise to attain the gidcy heights of the half way position There could he no two opinions as to which was the better balanced side in the game with Runcorn, yet the latter are to be congratulated upon giving their opponents ,such a good game. They are a very young side but seem to lack an experienced player in the back division to act as leader and to put the finishing touches on the movements. They had many oppor- tunities of scoring, but at the crucial time they lacked nish,and their efforts were wasted. Watton, though possessed of a good idea of what is required of a half- back, is not a Jolley.

The Runcorn forwards are a lusty lot, but unfor- tunately for them they caught the Warrington six at the top ( f their form. In the loose rushes they failed in comparison with \Val rington, who again dominated the bulk uf the play. More passing was attempted by the Warrington backs than in the Leeds match, but with little effect. Renwick ani Williams were the chief offenders. It was almost pathetic to see the bungling and wretched passing of these players. On 3evetal cccasions Williams missed the simplest of passes when the opposition had been overcome. Wednesday's display did not add to his reputation. Douglas, who was deputising for Daintith, spoiled his good work by trying to do too much on his own account. Nicholas played his best game f r Warring- ton, proving that he is a versatile player both on the attack and defence. Tranter and Davies formed the better wing, though their opportunities were fewer.

Of the forwards no one did better than G Thomas. considering the years " Ponty " has been playing football it is marvellous how he retains his form. Skelhorn is rapidly emulating his example and the

HUDDERSFIELD'S FINE DISPLAY.

The visit of the League champions to St Helens aroused great interest, and a large crowd of people assembled on the Knowsley road ground on Wednesday afternoon. They only saw a moderate game up to the interval, but afterwards Huddersfield gave them a fine exhibition, which was greatly admired, even though St Helens suffered a great deal from it. St Helens certainly did well up to half-time, and their fine forward play somewhat checked the Huddersfield com- bination. 0 eeson opened the scoring with an easy try, but Barton shortly afterwards Equalised the score. Just b -fore half-time Grey and Davies got away cleverly, and Davies scored a try,which Grey converted. At the interval St Helens were five points behind. They were however, quite overplayed all the second half,and bewildered by the brilliant passing of Hudders- field. Wagstaff was not quite up to his usual form, but still be gave Moorhouse many opportunities to show his fine speed. Huddersfield added 23 points to their interval score, and finally won by 28 points.

NORTHERN UNION CUP. SECOND QUALIFYING 'ROUND.

RYLANDS RECS. v. ST, HELENS wHITE ROSE.

A crowd of over '2OO 'witnessed this match on the Sherdley ground, St Helens, on Saturday. The teams had not met since the White Rose beat Rylands in the tinA for the Greenell Challenge Cup two years ago. White Rose being classed as the crack junior team ot St Helens. Itland3 losing the toss, played up the slope and began in earnest but Warren, from a pass by Horrocks, in trying to ground, lost the ball over theline. BARROW BACKS.

At Cavendish Park, Barrow, before 5,000 spectators Corcoran kicked off for Widnes against a fairly stiff breeze, and after a short attack by Gifford and Treloar, which was repulsed by Brassington, Taylor dribbled over the Barrow line froralialf-way. Dainteth's kick at goal failed. Thomas put the Barrow threequarters in possession, and Williams ran in a fine try, and Logan addtd the goal points. OUT-CLASSED IN THREE-QUARTER LINE.

St. Helens, who had the first use of the wind, ought to have scored if the backs had shown any ability to put to practical use the good work accomplished by the forwards. Warrington's Decisive Victory.

The match between Warrington and Runcorn was a atr.ng New Year's Day attraction and a crowd of about 5.000 lined the Wilderspool enclosur.. There was one change in the Warlington team which did duty against Leeds, Douglas taking the place of Daintith at half back. On the Runcorn side 13 Hughes appeared at half- back in place of A Haxworth. Teams : Warrington Jollev. full-back ; Davies, Tranter, Renwick (captain), Williams, three-quarter backs ; Nicholas, Douglas, half-backs ; Skelhorn, Cox, Cheaters Fearnley, G Thomas, Naylor, forwards. Runcorn : George Snaw, full-back ; W Newall, A Hughes, R Youd, C Thornton, three-quarter backs ; B Hughes, S Watton, half-backs ;A Richardson, J A Peacock, T amoghue, C Inman, R Massey, R. Paabury, forwards.

Referee: Mr Renton, gunslet touch judges: Messrs Valentine (Salford), and Lowe (13rougbton Rangers). By dribbling, the Runcorn forwards rushed play to the Warrington quarter-line. whereJolley made a daring save. Yond tried to break through, but Tranter accounted for him near the line. A passing movement between B Hughes, A Hughes, and Newell ended in the latter being well tackled by Davies. From tbe next scrimmage Wstton tried to break through, but he was well heln inside the line. At this stage Ruriourn were having the better of matters, and the Warrington line had narrow escapes. Shaw had his kick charged down by Fearnley, and the ball was !sent over the Runcorn line. Three Warrington forwards followed in hot pursuit, and Shaw, slipping under the post, Fernley dashed up and scored a try. Renwick added the goal. Undaunted, Runcorn again made a vigorous onslaught on the Warrington line. Youd and Thornton combined effectively, and Inman joining in the movement, the Runcorn forward was only pulled down under the post. The danger was cleared by a grand run by Williams, who dribbled the ball past Shaw' and looked ail over ' a scorer, when tiughes saved the situation under the post. A minute later, from passing by the Warrington backs,, Williams was knocked Into touch by Shaw clese to the line. Warrington bad another chance when Skelhorn charged down a kick, but was unable to recover the ball. Davies, on the other wing, was floored by Newell when looking dangerous. At this point Runcorn were severely pressed until B Hughes made a mark close to the line. A score was forthcoming, when Fearnley, feinting to pass to Shaw, he completed the effort with a try at the corner. Jolley failed at goal. Half-time, Warrington, 8 points ; Runcorn, nil. On resuming Warrington pressed, but Williams failed on three occasions to take advantage of exceptionally good openings. On one occasion he clean missed a pass from Renwick when the opposition had been over- come. A dribble by Nicholas and Skelhorn caught the Runcorn defence in a tangle, Skelhorn dropping on the ball over the line for a try. Nicholas added the extra points. A minute later Cadbury kicked the ball dead, and Cox was in the act of grounding. Runcorn had a chance when Youd, the Chester Collegian, intercepted a pass, but Tranter floored him near the post. Near the cloae G Thomas and Skelhorn added to Warrington's score, Renwick kicking a goal. Massey also scored for Runcorn, Padhury adding the goal. Result :—Warring- ton 21 points, Runcorn 5 points.

Last.' MINUTE WIN.

BROUGHTON RANGERS' BELATED VICTORY

OVER WIDNES.

The 4,000 people who assembled at Wheater's Field on Wednesday were provided with a thrilling and exciting game: Play was of a fluctuating character,and the Rangers only gained the victory in the last minute. At the outset offside play spoiled a Rangers' attack, and Widnes from the ensuing penalty kitSt made a raid on the home line where Lally had a grand opportunity but passed instead of going through on his own account. A feature of the Broughtonians' play was the splendid tackling of their forwards. A passing movement between Tighe, Barlow, Meade and Davidson was nipped in the bucliby Brassington, who punted strongly. Widnes then attacked, and a mistake by Denson let in Reid, who scored a smart try. The same player failed at goal. The visitors were eventually penalised, and Barlow was successful in kicking a goal for the Rancers.At half-time Widnes led by three points to two The second half was keenly contested, and though the, b angers attacked fiercely the visitors' defence was so sure that for k long time little impression was made on it. Widnes were prone to get offside, and twice Barlow made abortive attempts to kick a goal.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY JANUARY 4, 1913.

The moving finger writes, and baying writ Moves on; nor all thy piety nor wit Shall lure it back to cancel half a line.

So "old Khayyam " THE NEW YEAR. hymns the inexorable

march of time. The breaking of a New Year gives occasion to new hope and new desire—or perhaps one

should say to the renewing of old hopes and desires clouded and forgotten in the clash of events and the turmoil of strife in the twelve- month that is gone. In the fancy of man-- given by habit of mind to sentiment—New Year's Day provides an opportunity of pause and reflection. The world, as it were, stands with shaded eye peering into the future, and trying to make out what is in store for itself. "The moving finger writes," and man well knows that what is there set down cannot be erased; it stands for all time. But out of the past new visions rise. The trials of the finished year inspire the trust of the coming. "God's in His heaven, all's right with the world," is the message which rings out above the record of troubles conquered, and helps to keep man's spirit alive with the determin- ation to win to better things in the future. That is the spirit of youth and hope, and, standing on the threshold of a New Year, youth and hope are masters whom none deny. There are those among the prophets and seers who would have us believe that because the numerical round has brought us again ' to a " 13 " in the tale of the years fate must be full and perhaps unhappy for the world. The superstitious regard" 13" as an unlucky number; others say that in history, at any rate, the " 13's " among the years have been big with events. So Mr Frederic Harrison has been recalling that a hundred years ago 1813 saw the overthrow of the Napoleonic ambition, and yet a hundred years before that witnessed the collapse of the vaulting scheme of Louis XIV., the Grand Monarch. Europe was then recast; and in this year of grace, the advent of which we greet, Europe again will be recast quite certainly. For the Turk cannot longer maintain foothold upon those territories which five hundred years ago he conquered by the sword, and which, alas! for five hundred years he has ruled with sword and fire. So the New Year means new hope for the Christian peoples who at last are to be free. And, happily, there is no cloud upon the horizon to make us fear that when peace comes to the Balkans its ccming may be marred by a greater war, involving vaster issues for the nations of Europe. Diplomacy, directed happily by SIR EDWARD GREY, has dispelled the fear that existed of a European conflagration. So for the world there is no need to fear t hat 1913 will prove disastrous among the years. To us in England the warning voice is so often given to prepare for buffets from our neighbours that perhaps we may have grown indifferent to it. But there is, at any rate, a ground for confidence in the signs and portents which are permitted us to-day that we shall go through the next year as we ha ye through that which is gone h appily immune from the horrors of war.

At Lcme the prospect is not less bright In the Parliamentary arena a new weapon will for the first time come into actual use— the Act which limits the Veto of the House of Lords—and there will be much curiosity to see its effects in operation. Within a few days from now the great boon of State Insur- ance will be bearingiits first fruits for the poor and bringing the blessings of medical atten- tion to thousands of stricken homes where before would have been helplessness and despair. The great profession upon whose devotion and loyalty the working of Mr Lloyd George's Act depends will not, we are con- fident, fail to do its duty to the nation. Already the medical benefit for two- thirds of the insured persons is secure, and before the 15th of the month we believe the service will be completed. New problems will arise, new discoveries will be made, doubtless, in the year to come. "Man is permitted much to scan and !earn in nature's frame" wrote a great Englishman, and though he gave us the warning that even when fully skilled we still grope "in twilight dim" he taught us to trust for guidance ever to that "kindly light" which shines before all men.

Looking before and I A NEW YEAR after, the Liberal Party ANTICIPATION, sees the New Year in and the Old Year out with confidence and determination. As the »champion of Free Trade, [democratic princi- ples, a conciliation policy which spreads loyalty from end to end of a world wide Empire, the Liberal Party never was in a stronger position. An unexampled year of industrial prosperity proves that Free Trade And Liberalism are the best conditions for all classes of the community. And a policy of Social Welfare" opens out vistas through which wo all, whatever our politics, can ,gaze with sanguine hope, believing that if legislation and administration can be pursued on the lines of the last year, it will be better both for ourselves and the next generation.

The search for the THE DEADLY "deadly parallel" is • PARALLEL. seldom so richly . rewarded as in reading -the article in which the "Morning Post" endeavours to stimulate the courage of 'Tariff Reformers to face the food taxes. In -the same column we are told: And let us begin by In the meantime they isaying that the Dominions offer a preference in the scorn and repudiate the most valuable market in idea that we should sacri- the world, and we can dee anything for their sake. secure this preference, not only for ourselves but for our children and for ever, by making ai fair return, by offering reciprocity. That is the material. side of what we are asked to give up. If we have sons, have • they no claim upon us? We are not to sacrifice anything, but we are to give up something, in satisfaction of the claims that our sons have upon us! That something is to be "a preference upon Colonial foodstuffs and Fuch other products as may be thought desirable to include." We congratulate our contemporary (says the "Westminster Gazette.") It has managed -to make all the points of the different schools of Tariff Reformers, and, without regard to the fact that they cancel one another, to offer them to the same audience.

The very alarming rate THE BABY AND of infant mortality THE NATION, which has prevailed, especially in our great industrial centres, has during the past few years been the subject of much anxious atten- tion on the part of various public authorities, and a good deal has been achieved by wise measures for the protection of the baby from the ills which it is within the power of the State to guard it from. The death-rate,how- ever, is still far too high, and the suggestion is made, amongst many others, that a system of inspection of babies should be instituted. There is truth, certainly, in the argument that the inspection of schoolchildren fre- quently reveals the fact that boys and girls are suffering from disease which should have been attended to long before the child attained school age. The day has gone by for talking nonsense in this connection about Nature's work in weeding out the unfit. The great proportion of infantile mortality takes place, not as the result of natural law, but rather as the result of unnatural and • 'unhealthy conditions for child life. The rate is highest where the standards of comfort, of cleanliness, and of wages are lowest. At the same time, there is a decided feeling abroad that the inspector of this, that, and the other body, or thing, is becoming, to put it bluntly, an intolerable nuisance. Yet it is difficult to see bow effective action towards reform of any evil can be taken withn t him. The important consideration, of ( ,urse, is to ' -direct his activities and the activities which follow as the result of his inspection along the right path. To inspect and doctor a baby, and leave the problem, for instance, of its unhealthy and squalid environment untouched, is to have all the bother of the inspector and to experience no lasting good as the result of his efforts. In fine, the Problem of infantile mortality is not one for the inspector of babies alone. More rigorous and fearless enforcement of the existing laws of sanitation, together with housing reform, might, indeed, render the inspector of babies an unnecessary evil.

L.- NOTES AND NOTIONS. The Tory notion of " compensating " the working classes for Tariff Reform food-taxes is to take half-a-crown from them, and to give them back a shilling! No thank you'

*- * * Our " Legal Column" is now at the service of perplexed " Examiner " 'readers. We hope they will confidently refer their queries to our legal expert, and see that they Teach the office not later than Tuesday in each week. _ _

* - "Free Trade is not only a peacemaker and s good economist; it is a fine merchant, a ;good housewife; but it is a better doctor. Under Free Trade, pauperism is reduced, Parliament has been purified, commercial life cleaned, health advanced, and civic life iimproved."—Mr. Burns. * *

" 0 - Mr. Thomas Blades is the oldest member of -the Oddfellows in the world. He has recently passed his ninety-seventh birthday,but takes daily walk and looks very hale for his great age. Mr. Blades became an Oddfellow on February 3rd, 1840, when he joined a lodge, which is now extinct at Apethorpe. His 'name appears in the old rule Wok belonging -to the lodge, which is now in his possession. Mr. Blades maintains the keenest interest in iflddfellowship.

* -- Among his numerous other honours and -titles, Mr. Asquith is now entitled to the rank of Captain. This is by virtue of the Royal Warrant granting the rank of Captain to the Elder Brethren of Trinity House, and giving them precedence after Captains in the Navy. The Prime Minister has always shown a great interest in Trinity House since he was made an Elder Brother; and he nearly always wears their uniform on ceremonial occasions. Besides the great interest that he has always shown in naval matters, Mr. Asquith is the best sailor in the Cabinet and likes a holiday on the sea. Mr. Balfour will also be entitled to the rank of Captain in the future.

"

The Examiner, satui

Music-lovers have long had a grievance in the unsatisfactory Atte of the Parr Hall organ. They will therefore be pleased to note that the Parr Hall Committee has been instructed to consider and report upon the provision of a new instrument. This does not take things very far, but we hope the ultimate outcome will be the erection of an organ worthy of the building. * A curiosity of the Post Office illustrates the admirable excess of care bestowed upon his Majesty's mail's by this branch of the Government service. It was only a news- paper, and it was addressed to a certain village in -Yorkshire in which no geniuses have so far been born. The postal district was indicated in the address by the initial capitals "S. 0." The "0 " had been indifferently formed. It was read as "A." The result was that the unoffending news- paper was punished by being despatched on a journey and voyage to South Africa. No such postal district being found in South Africa, it returned duly, after having travelled many a weary mile, to its destina- tion in Yorkshire.

* Liberals have rarely been happier, or found more entertainment in the plight of the Opposition. In another column Mr. A H. Crosfield pertinently satirises the inter- nicine difficulties of the Tory Party in regard to food-taxes, while Mr. C. E. Parker puts some pretty posers to Alderman Bennett, the President of the Women's Tariff Reform League. That conscientious but irrational politician must frequently feel like a fish out of water. It seems a pity that his Imperial ideals should be harnessed to the dead weight of Tariff Reform and its nebulous propositions. He cannot but be aware how far the lariff Reform agitation has drifted from the conceptions of Chamberlain and Balfour. Perhaps Mr. Parker's reminders will help Mr. Bennett to a reconsideration of his, in many respects, anomalous position. * Have you noticed that Tariff Reformers propose to tax the necessities, and not the luxuries of life? Diamonds, of course, are raw materials, and so will escape taxation under the proposed Protective system. But flour and meat and fruit and butter and eggs and clothes and boots and furniture are necessities, and those things are to come under the price-raising Tariff. This is not a new thing for Protectionists to do. When the Americans last " revised " their tariff they took the import duties off mother-of- pearl, fossils, dried acorns, skeletons, turtles and such things; but they left the taxes on toys, clothing, tools, furniture, and crockery, —in short, on all the ;implements of labour and the necessities of the home. Protection is like that. Unless the articles protected were necessities they would not he bought, and what would become then, of the huge profits that are expected by those few manu- facturers who think they stand to gain under Protection?

OLD FOLK'S TREAT.

Happy Gatherings at the Parr Hall. Amid the joys of Yuletide, Warrington is always mindful of its veteran toilers, and the annual treat to the old folk has become such a firmly- established institution that to allow it to lapse would be unthinkable. That the true Christmas spirit, beloved of Charles Dickens, is still a potent force in our midst, is proved by the fact that public generosity enables the aged poor to be entertained on a really sumptuous scale at least once a year. Had the many who contributed their quota to the funds been able to witness the treat at the Parr Hall on Thursday they would assur- edly tiave felt doubly repaid by the gratitude of the guests. "Fit for the King" was the descrip- tion which. one veteran applied to the dinner served to the old tlen at noon. Certainly the fare was of the best, consisting as .it did of the good old English dishes of roast beef and pork, and plum pudding, with an allowance of beer or mineral waters as the diners preferred. About 500 par- took of the meal, and a motley—and in some respects pathetic—assembly they presented. There were bowed and weather-beaten men to whom life had evidently been a grim struggle, with few compensatiqns. Others, although carrying a weight-, of advanced years, had been dealt with 'more gently by Father Time, and, still looking hearty and vigcirous, they obviously took a pr,ide in their spick ,and span appearance. Mr. John Drury once more: occupied the honoured position of the oldest guest. A tall,.white-haired figure, he is in his .eighty-ninth year, and. delights in draw- ing upon his vast store of. interesting reminis- cences. At the head of an adjoining table was• Mr. Isaac - Johnson, who is eighty-five years of age, and has at many successive treats voiced the thanks of the old men to the organisers and sub- scribers. The -dinner was served under the direc- tion of Mrs. Powis, and a large. band of workers busied themselves attending to the wants of the assembly. The respected President (Alderman James Smethurst); was, of course, on the scene, arid by his geniality and good fellowship helped

The usual New Year's dance, under vie kind patron- age of Major M C A Green, Commanding Depot South Lancashire Regt, and Major V T Bailey, Commanding Depot the King's Regt, was held in tie Gymnasium, Orford Barracks, on Tuesday night, and dancing went on until the early morning of the New Year. The gymnasium was brilliantly decorated, and what with the pretty dresses and bright uniforms, the spectacle was really a fine one. At 12 midnight, all present joined hands and sang "Auld lang syne," after which a rather interesting ceremony occurred, the New Year I being let in by the new guard, comprising the threel boys Crotty, of the lb yal Hibernian School, under the command of Sergeant-Ilajor Powner,South Lancashire Regt, with Colour-Sergeant Thempson as Drum Major. The old guard representing 1912, under the command of Sergeant-Major A El Hatton, the King's Regt, challenged their right of admittance, but when they replied 1913 they were told t„) advance. After the usual salutes the old guard marched out. Great credit is due to the committee under Sergeant-Major Powner for the able manner in which everything was carried out.

Walker and Willett were associated in that capital duet, "The Battle Eve." Ataalsaac Johnson seconded, and expressed the lilt that the coming year would be one of the most prosperous they had ever experienced. The dinner had been "fit for the King," and their sin- cere thanks' were due to all who had so nobly pbevielt(l for their wants. They were particularly indebted to the President, than whom no man in Warrington was more deserving of their res- pect. (Applause.) They hoped he would live long to fill the post which he occupied with so much credit, for they could not have a better Pre- shrent. (Applause.) The vote was heartily passed, the company rising and Singing "For he's a jolly good fellow." iA short entertainment followed. Mr. J. Drury (son of the oldest guest) gave a stirring recita- tion "A Sgldier and a Man," an incident of the Crimean 'War; Mrs. E. A. Sanders sang "The mild plaid shawl" in her usual expressive man- ner. and Mr. Ernest Walker was heard to dis- tinct advantage in "Simon the Cellarer." which went with a capital swing. Mr. Albert Trow was an efficient accompanist. There was another happy gathering in the even- ing when 700 aged women sat down to an ,excel- 'rot tea, consisting of sandwiches, cakes, &c. The oldest guest—Mrs. Coffee—is 'eighty-nine. years of age.

PANTOMIME AT THE HIPPODROME.

" Robinson Crusoe."

Large houses will no doubt be the rule at the Hippodrome next week, for we are to have a visit from Messrs M Montgomery and E C Jazon, who will present Jazon's gorgeous comic pantomime "Robinson Crusoe." A number of popular music hall stars are included in the caste and there will be several specialities including the One by the Adams, comedy cyclists. There will also be a fish and sea- weed ballett. An augmented orchestra and chorus will be under the direction of Albert E Slack. The whole will be produced under the personal supervision of Mr E C Jazon.

PANTOMIME AT THE ROYAL COURT THEATRE. Next week we are to receive a visit from Mr Gar Kiddies' successful pantomime " Babes in the wood." This is Mr Kiddies' first visit to Warrington with any pantomime. The book is written in a merry vein of light humour, and is from the practised pen of J L Barry. The music embraces all the latest vocal successes. The scenery is on an elaborate scale and several pretty sets are assured. Miss Eva May Rosslyn will appear as Robin Hood. She has an ideal stage presence and will win the favour of the house at once. Her principal number is " Who were you with last night ? " which goes with a delightful swing. Miss Marion Duncan is the principal girl, and the Wee Sisters Wright appear as the " Babes" Harold and Lucy. The cast is rich in comedians, Mr Dawson Dewick as the Baron, Mr Dan Lyall as the" Dame" Araminter Koffeade, and the Brothers Slater are the villains of the stoty. The pantomiwe is to be pre- sented twice nightly, the first house commencing at 050 prompt. There will be a special matinee on Saturday at 2 30. Patrons are advised to book seats early.

===== 0002601_19130104_art0071.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DOCTORS AND THE ACT.

A PANEL FOR WARRINGTON.

An efficient panel for the administration of the medical benefits of the Insurance Act is assured for Warrington. Twenty-two medi- cal men, out of about thirty in active practice, have signed a provisional agreement accepting service under the Act in the Warrington area and this number is expected to be ample to work the Act in an adequate manner. Early in the week there was some difficulty because, while sixteen doctors signed a document intimating their willingness to sPrve, they reserved the right to accept or decline the agreement up to January 7th. The Insurance Commissioners were approached to see if such a conditional undertaking could be accepted, but they announced that the panel would only be kept open until to-day (Saturday) on con- dition that the doctors gave a written agree ment in the meantime, accepting service. The agreement which twenty-two doctors have ' nowsigned is as follows: "I agree to accept service on the panel for the Warrington area, under the National Insurance Act." This agreement, like those in all other areas, is provisional, for three months. On Thursday, the Mayor of Warrington, as Chairman of the local Insurance Com- mittee, attended the conference between the Chancellor of the Exchequer and the Advisory Committee. WORKINGMEN'S COMMITTEE AND

===== 0002601_19130104_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ACT.

A meeting of the executive of the Work- ingmen's Infirmary Committee was held at the Infirmary ont Tuesday to receive a state- ment from Mr. J. H. Smethurst, chairman of the Board of Management and Dr. Edward Fox, secretary of the Medical Board, relating to the work of the Infirmary with regard to the treatment of insured persons. Many important questions were put to Dr Fox, and considerable discussion took place upon his answers, but the matter was left in abeyance pending further inquiries.

CROSS COUNTRY RUNNINU.

The lull which u--dially marks holiday week and the turning point of the season accounted for the non- competitive nature of many club fixtures. Warrington A.C. had a more numerous pack watching the local forwards outshine the Leeds lot than either of the two packs which Shadwell led home after a five miles run at a useful pace. Owing to the Midland C.C.A. experiencing some difficulty in fixing the venue for the" National," arrangements for the other championships have been quite unsettled, but, if report speaks truly, it has been decided to recommend Danstall Park Racecourse, Wolverhatnptr n, so that preparations for the principal fixtures shout° now take more definite form and club officials will Low be looking up " ways and means." The locals have plenty to do before the season cicses, with the International which should take place in France at the end of March, as a glance at the following fixtures will show : January 11th Inter Club race with Runcorn at Warrington, in which the deciding round of the points competition takes place and a medal is in store for the first novice home of the local club; January ISth Novice Teams Race at St Helens, February 15th West Lancashire Senior and Junior Championships at Lancaster; and, if the teams qualify, March Ist Northern Senior and Junior Champions at Stockton, March 15th National Championship at W olverhatnpton, and there will also be a further race fur intermediate teams, on a suitable date, at St Helens. When the affiliation fees, entry fees, and other expenses indispensable to competitions at such remote points are considered, and it is remembered how the exchequer has been depleted, to provide their pavilion, it becomes evident what the local management have to do to provide opportunities for a working class membership, and a great deal depends on the measure of support accorded to the Treasurer.

hoto F. Mounfield. REUBEN BENNETT.

Since Beuben Bennett took office, three years ago much has been done, with a minimum of expenditure to provide clean healthy sport for the youth of the good old town of Warring.on, which has been kept well in the forefront of amateur athletics, and at this juncture it may be parmissible to recount his doings las an example. An ardent clubman, in more senses than one, never over confident but careful almost to a fault he is a firm believer in the principle ol' basiness first " to which may be attributed the fact that his track experiences have been almost entirely confined to holidays and mid week sports; although a footballer and swimmer of no mean part his versatility, particu- larly on the running path is probably better known abroad than at home 3 possessor of a useful turn of speed and sr. —..id finishing power he has on occasions greatly impressed the cognoscenti with a style and action which his build suggested would produce a nailing 'riddle distance runner and with Anno Domini not yet unfavourable there is no reason why this opinion should not be verified even after a temporary retirement. In cross country races he was not fortunes favourite and his most notable performances are second to llerbert Holbrook when the iuteinational was breaking his novice by winning the club race in 1900, first in the Christmas handicap in the same season and second n the similar event the following year, a useful

ho

position in the West Lancashire Junior of 1910 was a material factor towards the local team getting second position. His only appearance in a track team race was over two miles at Swinton in 1908 and the result will be memorable to all who saw the race in which Warrington ran four, G Watmough, R Bennett, H K Jepson and W Antiobus, and that they finished almost abreast in the first four positions, with the nearest opponent over 50 yards away, was largely due to a terrific burst by Reuben who came from a forlorn ryssition in the last 300 yards) Even a number of the competitors paused on the other side of the track to see the finish. On the track in August 1907 a second in the half mile at Rainford was followed by a first in the 440 yards at Widnes Police and first in the Tradesmen's 100 yards, off scratch, time 11 seconds, at Wai rington 'Police spolt ; his next appearance was Whitsun, 1908, when he won the half mile at Worksop and a 3rd in a 220 at Manchester, then during his 10 days holiday in August he squandered a field in winning a mile race at Liverpool, 2nd in the mile at Rochdale he made amends the next day, by winning the mile at Warrington Police and upset a " hot-pot " with such another finish as at Swinton which was the day but one following, and but for running an ill-judged race at Widnes Police a fitting climax would have been reached in the half-mile wherein he ran second after a finish which evoked the hearty hsndshike and con- gratulations of the Olympic hero, Melvin Sheppard, who finished third after being on terms with over a hundred yards to go. On Good Friday, 1909, he was entered at Burnley in both the half-mile and mile wherein the before-mentioned " hot pot" was the " goods" but the "double snip" came completely undone as Reuben won the half in convincing style, his clubmate, Arthur Siddall, won the mile and Burnley's comment was "on Warrington chaps can run." Another Whit NlenClay at Worksop resulted in a repetition of the half-mile win, in lmin. 55 2 ssecs. off 42 yards on grass; August Bank Holiday found him waiting too long and finishing second in the quarter mile at Retford. _ _ .

On Good Fliday, 1910, he waq again at Burnley but found giving Joe Driver (who has since run prominently and as a professional) 60 yards start and running through a big field in the mile, a proposition which left him with second prize as a memento; unable 0 train he decided to stay at home for Warrington harities festival and he annexed a third in the half mile, then he vient to Lytham and won the half mile off 45 yards in the excellent time, for the track, of lmin. 58secs. In 1911 he had only two outings 3rd in the 220 at Worksop, and 3rd in the quarter at Maltby, since when he has only been able to find time for an cccasi-mal spin just to keep the rust oil'. His carefulness never allowed him to fancy his chance, but he has nevertheless, always been a sound trier who has deserved his truly wonderful proportion of successes.

SEQUEL TO WORKS THEFTS. CARTERS AND MARINE STORE DEALERS

FINED.

Much interest was centred in several ens( s of theft from Messrs Pochin's, Ltd. which were heard at the Warrington Police Court on Thursday by Mr J Rymer Young and other maeistrates.—Frederick Machell, 77, Fox-streeti; W Gibney, 10, Wakefield-street; and William Nichols labourer, 12, Edward-street. were charged with steeling from the works of Mesrs Pochin Ltd., Bank Quay, 4cwt. of lead during the month of Deceniber.—Machell was further charged with stealing seven corrugated sheets, a bag of lime and other property of the firm to the value of £2. The Chief Constable stated that recently there was a fire at Messrs Pozhin's works and Macke!' and Gibney, who were employed by a local firm as carters were engaged to take away the refuse. Nichols had been employed by the firm and had the privilege of picking up any scrap metal. Gibney and Machell had been removing the stuff arid banding it over to Nichols, who sold it to a marine store dealer named Owen, of Lovely- lane. The second charge against Machell was that he had taken material f,om the works whilst engaged in carting refuse. He had removed it to his house. Machell was fined 40s including costs and the other two defendants were mulcted in a fine of 30s each, including costs. Francis J Owen, marine store dealer, Lovely-lane, was summoned on three charges of perchasing lead uncle' the prescribed weight as certified in the Act and further summoned for failing to keep the necessary books. Mr F R Roberts defended and pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said the Corporation had issued a licence to the defendant by which he could act as a marine store dealer. The Act compelled him to keep is record in his nook of all purchases of metal made by him. The present case arose out of tne last prosecution when this three men were charged with stealing lead. The police received information from Messrs Pochin's• to the effect that they were missing lead. After inquiries, they bad an interview with the defendant but he denied having purchased any lead from the men. The police were no satisfied end later they disoovered that Mr Owen had sold large quantities of three and four owts., to Joseph Matthews of Pump Yard, Lythgoes- lane. When informed of this Mr Owen again denied having sold any lead to Matthews. Mr Owen was later asked to produce his books and it was found that no entries had been made relatiug to the purchases of metal from the three men, Machell, Gibney and Nichols. Defendant was supposed not to buy less than one cwt. of lead at a time. Mr Roberts, for the defence, said his client had not been a marine store dealer long and knew very little about the trade. He bad done nothing with criminal intentions. With regard to his general character, he was well-known and at the recent municipal election he was the unsuccessful candidate in Howley Ward. The Chairman : Is that a testimonial of character ? Mr Roberts : I give it you for what it is worth. Continuing,Mr Roberts said apart from that Mr Owen had for many years been connected with the Ragged school and bad been one of the hardest workers, supporting it largely out of his own purse. He sub- mitted that the defendant was ignorant of the statute and pleaded for leniency. He wished to express regret for his act, but unfortunately denied knowledge to the police. A fine of lOs and costs in each of the three first charges was imposed and defendant was ordered to pay 20s. including costs on the fourth charge. Joseph Matthews, Pump Yard, Lythgoes-lane was fined £5 for busing brass under the required weight and further for carrying on business as a marine store dealer without a licence. The Chief Constable said there could be no question but that the defendant had for some time past carried on business of that nature, purchasing metal. The police visited his premises with reference to the lead stolen from Messrs Pochin's Works which he had purchased from Owen. The lead was found on his premises and he gave every information to the police. Mr Steel for the defence, said that, unlike the defendant in the last case, defendant did not deny knowledge to the police but gave them every informa- tion in his power. He thought such an admission ought to ‘zo in his favour. He give the market price to Mr Owen for the lead which he purchased. It wa quite trite he had no licence but he (Mr Steel), was prepared to give an undertaking to the bench that the defendant would not carry on that business any further. In view of the undertaking the bench took a lenient view of the case and fined Matthew £5.

Borough Police Court

NOIs;DAY. —Before the Mayor (in the chair) Aldermen Bolton and Smethurst, tied Messrs S Wright, W

Roberts, W Furness and T Locker. _ SCENE IN A LODGING HOUSE.—Peter Rush, 10, :Stanley•street, James Glynn, 11, Stanley-street, and Thomas Crosby, 10, Stanley-street, were charged on remand with assaulting various persons at the lodging- house, No. 12, Etanley-street on Boxing Day.— Kush was first charged with assaulting a man named Archibald Mitchell. Mr Jenkins appeared for the prisoner, who pleaded guilty. Mr Jenkins said Rush was an exceedingly well-behaved and good workman, but on the night in question he had had some drink and there was a melee. Most people indulged in a little relaxation on that day and these men had relaxed and enjoyed themselves in it way. Prisoner's employer said he was a good workman and had never lost an hour during the last eighteen months.—Rush was ordered to pay the costs, 13s. 6d. Olynu was charged with assaulting Patrick O'Brien. Mr Jenkins appeared for the prisoner, who pleaded guilty.—Mr Jenkins said the assaults were the results •of the Christmas festivities.—The Chief Constable said that at about 11.20 p.m. on Boxing Day the three men went to No 12, Stanley-street, and their attention was drawn to the fact that they were in the wrong house, but they remained, and afterwards Glynn and Crosby assaulted O'Brien. Glynn got hold of him by the throat. O'Brien fell to the floor and prisoner held on to him. The row was continued in the street.—A fine of 2.05, including costs, was imposed.—Crosby was charged with assaulting Patrick O'Brien, Kate kliggin- son and Archibald Mitchell. He pleaded not guilty.— The Chief Constable said the men went to the house •and O'Brien remonstrated with them about something they had said, and the men thereupon broke up the furniture. O'Brien was assaulted by Crosby.—O'Brien, in evidence, said Crosby injured his ribs.—Kate Higginson said Crosby struck her with a stick on the back of the band while she was at the door. She fainted and later bad to go to the Infirmary, where five stitches were put in the hand. When the police came she was in a pool of blood. Crosby who admitted he was drunk, was fined 20s and costs.

A MIDNIGHT VISITOR ?—John Webb, shoemaker, of io settled address, was charged with stealing a watch, ,vercoat and other articles, the property of Alban Hoyle, shoemaker, 16 Matthew-street on May 14th 1912 —Prisorer pleaded guilty and the evidence against him was to the effect that he obtained employment with Hoyle and lived in. Early one morning it was alleged that he arose and absconded with the property. He was arrested in Leeds. When taken into custody and on the way from Leeds to Warrington, he stated that someone knocked at Royle's door about 1-0 on the morning in question. He let the man in and returned to bed. When he got up about 4-30 he saw that his mid- night visitor had gone off with the articles, the subject of the charge. Fearing that suspicion might fall on him, he cleared out. Webb was committed for trial to the Liverpool Quarter Sessions.

A QUESTION OF " CONTRACT."—J oho William Wardle, carrying on business at Chicago Buildings, Whitechapel, Liverpool, and residing at the Norton Arms,Warrington, was summoned for travelling on the Cheshire Lines Railway, from Warrington to Manchester and back, without paying his fare. Mr Pinfold, who prosecuted, said the defendant was asked for his ticket by an examiner named Radford, and he replied "Contract." The ticket examiner knew he had b contract from Liverpool to Warrington. and promptly askei him. "Does your contract now cover Manchester?" to which defendant replied, unhesitatingly, "Yes." Being dissatisfied Radford waited for him on the return journey, and taxed him with regard to the contract. Defenthnt then said he had another contract from Liverpool to London, which he supposed covered Manchester. This statement was found to be incorrect. Mr Browne, for the defence, submitted tlat his client had acted in a cireless manner. A five of 20s. was imposed. ST. HELENS FINANCES. 7 TWO COMWTTEES OVER-SPEND £23,000:

The St. Helens Corporation's Water Committee has decided to apply for sanction to borrow £4,658, amount overspent on Nielling pumping station, and £5,000 for new mains and services during the coming five years. The Gas Committee has overspent 413,787 on capital account and £4,700 on the suspense account, a total of £18,187, and has ,decided that the £5,00 in the reserve fund be used to reduce this amount, and that the balance be paid out of revenue during the next three years.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

" The Collier's Lass."

The true note of homely sentiment is pleasingly struck in Mrs F G Kimberley's great domestic drama, " The Collier's Lass " which has been presented so successfully at the Royal Court Theatre this week by Mr J Newton Cowling's Co. A large measure of sensationalism assures success in the "popular " parts of the house, but this element is modified to some extent by the witty humour which is kept running throughout the Play. The scenic effects are capital, that depicting the colliery disaster,being very realistic. The story is good in all its simplicity and the sympathies of the audience soon go out to the colliers lass, who through her desire for dress and ainuseinen t acts rashly. There is the anguish of the parents, the search by the lover, the cunningness of the villian in avoiding detection, the colliery disaster, the return of the girl to the pit side to see if her father is among those doomed, and as a finale, repentance,and re-union for the collier's family, and the villain's usual reward. Miss Nina Blake Adams is at once a favourite in the title role and deserves the praise lavished on her for her finished and sympathetic interpretation of the character. Ur Kenneth Jackson makes the character of Frank Cecil, the indulgent, unscrupulous villain, as repellent as possible by his clever style and facial expressions. As his accomplice and foil, Miss Hazel Hind puts plenty of spirit into the part of Maud Fernley, the cruel, heartless keeper of the house in London. She can be captivating and revengeful in turn, an accomplishment which ensures for her the execrations of the audience. The hall of humour is kept rolling by Miss Bella Moody and Mr Bert Johnson as 41ary Ellen Nuttall and Joe Maggins, two typical Lancashire factory people. Mr Edward Mackenzie has a just conception of what a scheming colliery owner should be and is highly successful as Herbert Cardil. Miss Ellen Beverley and Mr Henry Rutland act well as Mr and Mrs Copley, the parents of the collier's lass, although Mr Rutland's conception of the Lancashire pit baud lacks something in the way of natural roughness. Mr Stanley ltudcliffe achieves popularity as Tom Fielding, the ardent lover of the heroine.

===== 0002601_19130104_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
" UREAT ILLUSIONS."

Mr. Frederic Harrison Ridicules Tory Party.

Mr Frederic Harrison, in his yearly address to the members of the London Positivist Society at Essex Hall, London, on Wednesday night, said that in our time there was not only one "great illusion." There were many, and perhaps the greatest of all was the pros• pect of an Imperial defence being worked by en all- England joint company with unlimited liability. This bellicose form of the " great illusion " had its financial and commercial forms in a joint Imperial Budget, in which our colonial friends were to tell the British workmen what their food was to cost them and how much dearer foreign manufactures were to be. To this Imperial Treasury Board the Opposition told them TIO Indians or Irish were to be admitted. This "great illusion " of a pan-Britannic, semi-Asiatic, semi- A frican, semi-American confederation, meeting the world in arms seemed already to be producing ohaos in a great historic party, and he trusted it would never beguile Great Britain to its confusion and its ruin. The old country might be degraded, but it was not izati yet. (Laughter).

HOW TO MAKE ANARCHISTS. Scour the countries of Europe for able-bodied men and women who desire to better themselves. Fill them with hope by giving them glowing reports of what a wonderful place the United States is and of how easy it is here to attain wealth and affluence, together with personal liberty, equal rights, and exact justice.

- When they arrive make every effort to hnmble them by giving them low wages, by huddling them together in quarters unfit for human habitation and by gouging them on every side.

- -- Speak to them harshly and apply contemptuous names to them, such as "guineas" or " wops " If they complain or ask for better treatment, tell them they are only foreigners and should have stayed in their own countries.—" Life," U.S.A.

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

A splendid example of what the management of the hippodrome can do in the way of providing a first class entertainment, is seen in the fine holiday programme which has been submitted this week. The new year has been commenced exceedingly well at this popular vaudeville theatie and all music-hall lovers can rest assured that only the best and most up-to-date "turns" will be engaged to entertain the public. Artistes of quality and refinement, possessed of good voices, are included in Max Erard's company of vaudevillians who have received such a warm welcome at each per- formance. Much taste is shown in the choice of songs and each individual artiste is a " turn" in himself or herself. The company interpret a number of beautiful song scenas in the most elaborate of settings. Indeed the staging of the scenas is a feature of itself and it is not surprising that repeated calls for encores are made. Originality plays a prominent part in the humorous enteztainment provided by .Nixon Grey, a comedian with a distinct style of his own. His " Zig-zag " path i number is decidedly funny and well conceived. It is for this that Greys' visit to Warring- ton has been so much appreciated, for it is humorous in the extreme without the slightest inclination to vulgarity. Such turns are bound to please. The Three Virus are certainly past masters in the art of juggling and club .swinging, swiftness of execution and neatness, playing a prominent part in all their tranidektsh. Seluaerhgedaanmceorusnats otfhe Fa prpa Lsea gay trio accorded them atrare, each performance is strong evidence that such Artistes are favourite s with Warrington people. A wonderful exhibition of whirlwind dancing is given by the junior member of the troupe. The Piquays are highly successful in a refined, novel and amusing entert,iin- ment, A vocal and musical comedy act, "Regatta Day " is presented by Maude St Malo and Harry Coombs, the singing being good and the artistes proving themselves expert manipulators of the concertina. A laughable picture "A deal in crockery!' is shown on the bioscope.

MRS. DISRAELI.

In later days Mrs. Disraeli used laughingly to declare: " Dizzy married me for my money ' - but, if he had the chance again, he would marry me for love." She was a woman of many eccentricities, which did not diminish as she grew older ; but her self-sacrificing devotion te her husbani was so exemplary that it disarmed ridicule. " Dizzy," she once said to a lady still living, " Dizzy has the most wonderful moral courage, but no physical courage. When he lies his shower-hath, I have to pull the string." Disraeli rewarded her love by an absolute fidelity, and by a gratitude which he was never ashamed to avow. She died in 1872, and her hnsband said, " She was the most cheerful and most courageous woman lever knew," and " I never passed a dull hour in her company."—The Right Hon. G W E Russell in the "Contemporary Review" for January.

The young people looked to Santa Claus as the author of their festivals and, without speaking in a didactic way, he believed it would be a good thing if the older peopl e would see .in such bright occasions the hand of a divine Providence, and regard the generosity of th ose who had contributed to the presents as the adm;n istration of the gifts of Providence. The Mayor cone luded by wishing all present " A happy new year." (Applause.) Mrs. BROADBENT then presented the gifts and addressed a few kind words to each recipient. No one was forgetten for, in addition to the patients, the doctors, nurses and all the workers at the institution had their tokens of remembrance and appreciation. Gifts were afterwards taken to those patients whose health did not permit their attendance at the ceremony. The MAYOR made a sympathetic allusion to Dr Johnson. junior house surgeon, who sustained a serious accident on Christmas Eve (as briefly reported in last aturday's " Examiner.") He said the fact that the accident had occurred at Christmas time made it even sadder than it otherwise would have been, but be was pleased to siy that Dr. Johnson was making very good progress towards recovery. (Applause.) He had had a very miraculous escape. • Mrs BROADBENT, having handed out all the presents, expressed the great satisfaction which the performance of that little duty had afforded her and said she hoped the patients would also look back to the day with pleasure. She was sure they would feel that the institution was a splendid place in which to be made well and strong again. (Applause). Mr J H SMETIIURST proposed a vote of thanks to the Mayor and Mrs Broadbent. At the outset he mentioned that apologies for non-attendance had been received from Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., and Lady Greenall, the Rev Canon and Mrs Willis, Mr J Charlton Parr, Mr and Mr a F W Monk., Mr and Mrs W Long, Rev John and Mrs Yonge, Mr and Mrs G D,akin, and' Dr E A Fox. Continuing, he expressed his gratitude to all the subscribers who had helped by sending money direct to the matron or to himself, to the givers of toys and—what he considered highly important—to the managements of the various places of amusement for allowing him to appear' on their stages during the previous week. Only those who knew something of the working of theatres and music halls could fully appreciate the courtesy extended to him. He had really been the " star " turd (laughter) —at any rate he had absolutely topped the bill from the financial point of view. Not only the large places of amusement like the Royal Court Theatre and the Hippodrome, but all the picture palaces had helped to provide money for the infirtnary in the way he had mentioned. (Applause.) He wished cordially to thank the members of Messrs Crosfield's ambulances brigade arid he welcomed their presence that day as some slight recognition of the work they had most gladly done for him. It was no slight matter for the men who turn up night after night to take the collect- ing boxes round the places of entertainment to help to count the money and then to receive no reward but" thank you." (Applause.) Having acknowledged the valuable help of the matron and staff in making arrangements for that day's function, Mr Smethurst announced that the second part of the Christmas treat would take place on the following Wednesday when the out-patients would receive their gifts. Nearly 200 children under the age of twelve years were receiv- ing attention in the out-patients department and no-one I would be overlooked in the distribution of presents. Some people might be dispoaed to say "You have more money than you want" but in that, as in every other hospital, they would never get sufficient funds for their needs Whatever balance remained after pioviding the Christmas treat would. in the first instance, be devoted to raising the amount still outstanding on the purchase of the X-rays apparatu.. They had now appliances at the disposal of their staff which were second to none in England, for a hospital of that size —(applause) —and the cost had, of course, been con- siderable. They spent more money than was origin- ally expected and he promised that he would try to raise the balance of .f,65. He was pleased to say that he believed, when he had finished collecting at 11 o'clock that night, that he would not only have that £65, but would be able to give a small contribu- tion to the Samaritan Fund, Mr JOHN FAtaceouen (chairman of the House Committee) seconded and the vote was heartily accorded.

The MAYOR, in returning thanks, said he believed Mr Fairclough was the oldest ex-Mayor of Warrington now living. (Applause). During the afternoon tea was served and Mr E Wilkes's band played pleasing selections.

CHRISTMAS JOYS. GIFTS FOR PATIENTS AND STAFF

OF THE INFIRMARY.

PLEASING SCENES.

Even into the halls of pain and suffering the Christmas season brings its joys, modified though they may be by the strain of pathos which must inevitably show itself when people are confined to sick-beds, away from the home circle, during the great time of festivity. To make the patients forget the saddening side of their circumstances and surroundings, as far as it is humanly possible, has been the aim of the staff of he Warringt,n Infirmary this Christmastide. By prettily-contrived decorations the wards were trans- formed into places of colour and beauty and every- thing that kindly consideration could suggest was done to impart a feeling of cheerfulness and quiet enjoy- ment. The celebrations had their crowning point on Saturday 'afternoon in die shape of the usual Christmas tree and distribution of gifts. This eagerly-welcomed functi in owed its inception to Mr J El Smethurst, the energetic chairman of the Board of Management, Who each Christmastide is allowed to plead the cause of the Infirmary from the stages of the various places of amusement and to invite the audience to contribute to a collection for the provision of a Christmas treat. This year Mr Smethurst has felt justified in regarding himself as the " star " turn, if the monetary recognition is any criterion, for so successful has been hie missionary campaign that not only has the cost of the gifts been defrayed, but there is a substantial balance to hand over to the ,-;1-ray apparatus fund. Many ladies and gentlemen responded to the invitation to witness the distribution of gifts on Saturday afternoon, In the large vacant ward at the top of the new block, a huge Christmas tree had -been set up. It shone with coloured electric glow lights, glittered with tinsel, and its branches were weighed down with Christmas crackers, dolls, and other articles which delight the childish heart. The room itself had been nicely embellished with streamers and Chinese lanterns. But tuore cheering than anything else was the glow of happiness which lighted up the faces of those patients who were participating in the distriou- tion. They had been wheeled into the ward in their beds and, amid the brightness of the scene, their ailments were temporarily banished. Doe little fellow, practically helpless, who has been lying in the institution for twelve months, was bubbling over with the excitement of the occasion and amused everyone by the delightfully quaint way in which he prattled to nurses and visitors.

The presents -which were to be distributed were not hanging on the tree but had been neatly arranged in parcels. They were handed to the patients and staff by Mrs Broadbent, the s enior member of the Ladies' Committee. Amongst the visitors present were : —The Mayor (Councillor Dr Joseph), Mr J H Smethurst (chairman of the Board of Management), Mrs Rylands, Mr and Mrs W Peter Rylands, Mr F A Frost, Mr Roger C Parr, Alderman and Mrs Smethurst,Miss Smethurst,Miss M Broadbent, Alderman Burton (chairman of the 'Workingmen's Committee), Councillor and Mrs °Jackson Holmes, Mr John Fairclough, Rev H V and Mrs Pigot, Dr and Mrs Bowdon, Mr IT Collin, Dr Law (senior house surgeon) and Miss Hammond (matron). The MAYOR briefly alluded to the beneficent work of the Infirmary and said those who perforce bad to be patients had reason to congratulate themselves on being in such an efficiently managed institution—an institution which was thoroughly up-to-date from the scientific point of view and comprised the best medical and surgical art and the highest nursing skill. (Applause.) A festive occasion like that was one of the bright spots in the eventfol history, of the Infirmary, but it tended to illustrate in a pUblic form the human touch and the kindliness which accompanied tile treatment which was bestowed on the patients throughout the year. It was said that there was a silver lining to, every cloud and those celebrations might not inappropfiately be regarded in that light. He could not help thinking that one of the bars to true happiness was the lack of appreciation of the benefits which many people received. He was not saying that to induce the recipients of the presents that afternoon to express profuse gratitude, but he believed that one half of the battle with regard to happiness was a true spirit of thankfulness to the quarter from which evidence of kindly thoughtfulness came.
