TORY CONFUSION.

THE STAMPEDE FROM FOOD TAXES. Conversion of " Daily Telegraph."

Confusion becomes worse confounded in the ranks of the Tory party, for the conviction is gradually being brought home that there is not a shadow of a chance of winning the next General Election on food taxes. The " Daily Telegraph" is the latest Imperial Preference organ to demand the dropping of those very duties without which Imperial Preference is impossible. Party interests are unblushingly placed uppermost. Toryism is showing to-day, more eh arly than ever, that its first concern is not to " safeguard the trade of the Empirz " by imposing tariffs, but to get back to power. With this object in view, strong influences are now at work to get food taxes shelved, so that the party may appeal to the country unhampered by any policy of its own except that of getting rid of the presen t

Government. The country is to be asked to give the Unionitts blank cheque, as it did in the Khaki Election ( 1 1900. The mandate then was "to finish the South African War." It was interpreted afterwards as a verdict for an unjust Education Bill, which aroused the resent- ment of all Nonconformists, and a new monopoly for the liquor trade. On the next occasion, if the electors are foolish enough to give the oppottunity, the blank cheque will be filled in with food taxes. With the addition of the "Daily Telegraph" we have the following Unionist organs advocating the blank cheque policy, or arguing that food taxes are now highly improbable since Mr Bonar Law has thifted the responsibility to the 6verseas Dominions,

who will not accept it : " The Times." " Yorkshire Post." "Daily Mail." "Sheffield Daily Telegraph" "Evening News." "Glasgow Herald." "Daily Graphic." " Nottingham Guardian." "Saturday Review." "Irish Times" (Dublin). "Spectator." "Belfast Newsletter." "Liverpool Courier." "Northern Whig" (Belfast) Among these the degree of candour varies. The Liverpool Courier" is foremost in boldly advocating the restoration of the Referendum to allay the anxiety of Unionist Free Traders. Others are discreetly silent on this point, while the " Daily Telegraph," on the same lines as the" Daily Graphic," in advocating subsequent " ratification " by the country, does not commit itself to ratification by Referendum or a second General Election.

"RATIFICATION AFTER." HOW THE "DAILY TELEGRAPH" HAS BEEN

CONVERTED.

Saturday morning's "Daily Telegraph," annotacing its conversion to the policy of shelving food taxes, cculd not have been pleasant reading to the Tory leaders Conviction, in this case, seems to have hen driven home by the storm of protest raised in the Oversea Domini ns by Mr Boner Law's proposal to shift the responsibility from his own shoulders. it would be idle and dangerous (bays the "Tele- graph ") to shut our eyes to the fact that tbe view which regards this plan as throwing on the Dominions the onus of an unpopular decision is not, only held by many Unionists here, but is the inter- pretation almost unanimously put upon Mr Boner Law's words by commentators overseas. Such expressions from quarters intimately con cerued in the plan put forward in regard to food duties raise a very Eeriotta problem for the con- sideration of Unionist leaders. They will be the first to agree that this is not a time for Imperial misunderstandings. There are only two ways of obtaining the direct authority of the electorate for any legisla- tiveproposal. One is to seek it in the form of mandate beforehand; the other is to seek it in the form of ratification after the introduction of a measure, by appealing to the country upon that measure. Whether the one or the other of those methods be selected is a question to be determined by the nature of the proposal and other political circumstances of the case. The situation now is that, in regard to the possible imposing of food duties for the sake of Imperial Preference, Mr Boner Law has expressed himself in favour of the way of mandate befora- hand ; while an over-whelming body of evidence from Unionists in the closest touch with the con- stituencies is to the effect that the method of ratification by subsequent appeal to the country is what the occasion demands. More than one reason is advanced for the main- tenance of this view; but the most cogent and as we think, the most unassa;lable is this : The next General Election ought to be, ro!far as Unionists can make it so, purely and simply the expression of a national verdict upon the course of political profligacy to which the Government has abandoned itself in the pest two years. TARIFF REFORM WEAKNESS CONFESS El) The " Economist " contains the following out=poken article :—ln the last few days the weakness of the Tariff Reform structure has been confessed. The pro- moters are falling out, the subscribers are grumbling. and the edifice is crumbling. It may be remembered that when Mr Chamberlain started his raging, ptopa- ganda, over fifty "Free Fooders," as Mr Bowles called them, rallied to the anti-Protectionisst and anti- Preferential lhg. Hard work, mostly underground, with the spade and pickaxe, drove some of these into the Liberal party, ensured the defeat of others, and forced the majority into retirement. After the tre- mendous defeat of 1906, Mr Balfour found that practically the whole of his Parliamentary party was hound to do at least lip service to the new Chamber- lain creed of Protection and Iwerial Preference, including taxes on food. Verbal and nominal unity prevailed. The result would have been different if the Free Traders had carried into party practice the doctrine of retaliation. Had they menaced the Chamberlainites with Free Food Conservative candi- dates, and had they worked half as strenuously at their Unionist Free Trado League as Highbury did at its Tariff Reform League, they would never have been beaten down and hurnilitated. It would have been far better for the health of the Unionist party if a strong and obstinate fight had been put up by the genuine Conservatives when Mr Chamberlain proposed to write fiscal revolution on the party programme. It is now freely stated that less than a quarter of the Unionist Opposition in the House of Commons really believe in the policy of Tariff Reform Nor would it be at all surpris- ing if the majority of the Unionist Press were to leave the Protectionist faction before the end of the year. But that faction is still powerful. It controls the caucus and the chest. It pulls the wires, and it has shown is power by compelling the two leadens of the party to repudiate the Recereneum. Tariff Reform will die hard, but it has no right to -expect the Conservative party to die with it. If

===== 0002601_19130104_art0092.txt | included=true | included =====
ry 4,19 n.

organisation and money could have achieved the destruction of our Free Trade system it would have been destroyed in 1906 or 1910. But fortunately there i sufficient intelligence in the electorate to appreciate the overwhelming force uf the facts, figures, and argurnentg, which have utterly shattered the case put forward by Mr Chamberlain nine years ago.

lIR. BONAR LAW'S POSITION. THREAT OF RESIGNATION.

The " Pall Mall Gazette " gives prominence to a communication on the Conservative et Isis from a well- informed correspondent. The situaton, says the writer, is now so delicate that unless the Unionist party is more careful and patient, unless it shows a more prudent regard to the elementary conditions of cohesion, it may lose its leader in circumstances plunging it into chacs. In no circumstances will Mr. Bonar Lhw do anything whatever which would mean yielding to outside pressure. If there is to be any modification of policy it must come from himself after a proper interval, when the momentary excitement has cooled. When all Unionist interests, and even Unionist existence, are so evidently at stake, if there is to be any important modification or development of policy, it cannot be precipitate. It must be considered, deliberate, and final. Mr. Bonar Law is in manner and temperament the most conciliatory of men towards his own followers. His Ashton speech was in itself a proof of that. No leader has surpassed him in the desire to keep his party together, but there are strict limits to his endurance. From the first moment of taking over that heavy heritage of Unionist leadership which he has so brilliantly sustained, be has made it clear that be would not consent to hold that leadership one hour if it ceased to be compatible with his personal self- respect, his unblemished rublic credit, and his full moral authotity as head of the party—in the House of Commons. Therefore, if there is any attempt during the next few weeks to subject him to pressure of any kind or to hustle him into any modification of his Ashton speech it is absolutely certain that he will go.lf he does, good bye to Unionist hopes for many and many a year. Unity under any other leader would be, in present circum- stances, impossible. This is tie basic fact of the I situation.

STAMP DUTIES. List week's writer on "Somerset House and its work " had a pa:agraph under the sub-headline "The guinea stamp" (more Rober-r-t Butrens) which brought to my mind a conversation 1 recently heard ' betw en an estate agent and a client. Said the former: "I don't know what your politics may be, nor do I much care; I tell y( u Lloyd George is a very expen- sive luxury." The speaker used a much stronger phrase.than that, but I have toned it down consider- ably, for that sort of criticism has become decidedly stale, and is not only cheap hut nasty. However, the one so addressed asked Tor further explanation, and the excited Tory, producing a tenancy agreement, de, lared that only a few years ago the stamp duty on documents precisely of that character was sixpence only. He understood that in the meantime it had been quadrupled,and he had according'y prepared the agreement on a sheet of foolscap, bearing a 2s. stamp; but it had been returned with an intimation that the duty is now 4s. Hence the choleric little man's show of indignation, not to say wrath. CURIOUS EXISTING BEQUESTS. Last week's particulars as to some famous wills to be seen at Somerset House may here be supplemented by the following :—At Shennington, near Edgehill,the church is strewn with freshly mown grass every season in commemoration of the fact that the church was thus strewn at its consecration, it not being yet paved. To keep this old custom up, two local ladies bequeathed a piece of ground to grow the necessary grass. Every 6th April there is sold in con' ection with a local charity at Bourne, in Lincolnshire, the "White Bread Meadow," by curious auction fashion. At the beginning of the bidding two boys start on,a race, and the bidder at the moment when the winning boy breasts the tape is the tenant for the coming 3ear. After the per formance bread, raisins, and ale are pro- vided.At Whitsuntide there is annually sold:at Enderby in Leicestershire, the grass ,of part of the Rathy Meadow, this meadow having been granted ty John f Gaunt to fifteen men who sanwered his invitation to come to him at Leicester and receiNe from him a piece of land, as a commemoration of his joining in the local festivities of " mowing day." The only condition of his grant was that this particular portion should be reserved for annual sale, and the proceeds applied t ) charity. It is said that the annual sale has only been interrupted during three years of the Civil War period. On May Day a curious wedding is celebrated at St. Goorge's in the East, London, in accordance with the terms of a bequest by William flame, who died in 1742. By his will a sum of four thousand pounds was set aside, the interest of which was to provide a marriage-portion for a girl from his schools in Old Gravel-lane. For this prize six girls, at the age of 22, who had been brought up at these schools, were to draw lots from a tin tea-canister. The only conditions of the marriage were that the proposed husband was not t) 1-e a soldier or a sailor, must be of the Church of England, and be approved by the trustees. I daresay the expert who has arranged to ecl't a weekly legal column for the " Examiner " will grant that the following hints as to will-making are sound enough:— No figures or abbreviations may be made in a will. Everything should be written out at full length. A will need not assume any particular form. Any writing duly signed and witnessed by two witnesses will be held valid. Except by the marriage of a testator, a will can only be revoked by another will or a codicil inconsistent with the former. When a codicil, though not consistent with the former will, does not actually revoke it, the first will is valid as far as it is consistent with the latter will or codicil. NEW YEAR ITEMS. In drawing to a close, I very sincerely and heartily wish my readers a happy New Year. Too often has Christmas week been marred by some disaster of the kind, but New Year's Day has been comparatively free from railway accidents, though there was one in North Wales in 1883 on the first day of the year, when the train fell over a cliff that bad given way. I have just referred to the subject of wills, and it may here be observed that January Ist plays a large part in matters of law. Since 1751 it has marked the commencement of the legal year, and the majority of Acts of Parliament of a general nature come into force on this day. Will those Warring- tonians who have this week rung out the old and rung in the new as usual credit this story of the extra- ordinary forgetfulcess of the bell-ringers of a country parish in Yorkshire? For more years than anybody could remember that old and new year ringing had been the regularly-observed custom on the bells of the Parish Church. But on this occasion the bells were silent and the parishioners were much concerned at the omission of the time-honoured observance Inquiries were made as to the reason thereof, the explanation given being that the ringers had all forgotten that the time was at hand. I further read that the festivities of the season, or some other distraction caused the inhabitants of a certain Lancashire town to overlook an important duty which should have been discharged on New Year's Day, viz., the triennial election of the local school board some dozen years ago, nominations for which shoula have been put in by the first day of the New Year to conform with legal requirements. However, when that day arrived no nominations had been received, so there could be no election. This meant that the retiring members would continue to serve for another three years, if they bad no objection. It was not so long afterwards that the Tories abolished the school boards as such, and left the important work to over-burdened members of the Town Council—in my bumble judgment a tremendous error that has resulted in irreparable mischief.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

"HOW I BEGAN." Two columns in juxtaposition on page oof last Saturday's issue of this j urnal specially interested me for reasons which will in due course appear to those who care to read on—l mean the copyright article on "Somerset Hens° and its work" and the reproduction of Mr. George Edgar's "Tay Pay" con- tribution. Writing from memory and without handy means and reference, I am unable to repeat the story literally, but Mark Twain once said s =thief; like this: "Julius Oesar is dead, Napoleon is dead, Sbakspere's dead and I don't feel so very well myself" I am aware that the comparison I now make is equally absurd—nay, much more so; but, like Mr. Edgar, I began life in a lawyer's cffice. arid during my three years there saturated myself with Dickens. We passed into journalism about the same age i . The gulf between us has gradually widened; ihe s n the enjoyment of the honour and distinction helms Ei) well earned and deserved, while I continue the everlasting grind as a common or garden journalist—poor, unknown but very happy in my work and pi oud of my profession. TIMEKEEPING (?) The successful Warringtonian in question, who was formerly a member of the " Examiner " staff, refers to his experiences as a works timekeeper, and, as be looks back upon that part of his youthful career, remembers his faults—like the chief butler who in Joseph's time was restored to Pharaoh's favour and temporarily forgot his indebtedness to the wonderful interpreter of dreams whose fellow-prisoner be had been. I was reminded of a mechanical device for checking the more or less honest British workman. I saw this instilled in a big corn mill employing its own joiners. TUese men had to "peg in" their time of arrival each morning, at.cl the machine kept each individual's record. However, as subsequent events preyed, there was nothing to pre- vent those who happened to be in time "pegging in" for an absent fellow who, when the conditions were reversed, would return the favour. Moreover, I accidentally discovered that those who promptly pegged in for themselves did n t immediately proceed to work, but systematically chatted and smoked away the first half hour or so! As the manager of a huge provision store once said to me when I expressed admiration for an ingenious cash till arrangement just adopted, if one is deliberately dishonest and determined to get the advantage, there is no mechanical invention that is an _absolutely complete cheek. TO AUTHORSHIP FROM-- From Dickens downward, many popular writers have reached literary heights by way of journalism ; and fewer, Jerome K Jerome among them, have done so through the railway service. It was Mr George Edgar's lot to expel ience something of both. At least for a while be worked as a railway clerk. By-the- bye, that brilliantly gifted brother of the immortal Haworth parsonage trio, Bramwell Bronte, likewise had what Mr Edgar describes as his bi ief, inglorious career on the railway. For a time he was employed by the L. and Y. Co. as a booking clerk and served in that capacity at the small wayside station of Luddendenfoot, in the West Riding—a few miles beyond the East Lancashire border. lam quite familiar with the place, having worked in those parts, and I have often wondered if any admirer of his genius venerates it on that account. It is possible enough. Once I was in the company of two fellows who were discussing Burns—an enthusiastic Scot and " anither mon." The latter thought to disgust the Caledonian by telling him with solemnly wagging head that he knew of a refuse heap on which his darling poet had lain in a beastly state of intoxication and be was considerably startled when the Burns- lover begged to be informed of the precise locality so that be might hasten thither on pilgrimage ! THE GOOD OLD DAYS. Now let me turn to that companion column respeet- ing Somerset House. I know my Somerset House, and could many a tale unfold concerning it. My father was a clerk in the Admiralty department there, and as a youngster I played almost daily on the terrace overlooking the river and in the quadrangle

GOLDSMITH: A POET FOR LAND

REFORMERS.

By Wells Drury.

Did you evtr consider Goldsmith's poem, " The Deserted Village," what a thorough lesson it is for Land Reformers? Have you reflected on the warning it sounded against Lnonopolisation of land by a favoured few? Glance over the eloquent lines again at.d choose (ut the gems of wisdom. They are worthy of your attention. Though penned 144 years ago, these thoughts are as fresh and impressive as if uttered yesterday. The keynote is sounded almost at the beginning : Otte only master grasps the whole domain, And half a tillage stints thy uniting plain. The inevitable result is thus pictured by the poet., who was also a scer _

Sunk are tby bowers in shapeless ruin all, And the long grass o'ertops the mouldering wall ; And, trembling, shrinking from the spoiler's hands, Far, far, away, ti y children leave the land. Then follow these lines of light and ttuth, quoted so often that they appear trite, but they have not lost their force :

11l f,ires the land, to hastening ills a prey, Where wealth accumulates and men decay.

. . . a bold peasantry, their country's pride, When once destroyed can never be supplied. We can now begin to appreciate the retrospective vein in which the following was written : A time there was, ere England's griefs began, When every rood of grcund maintained its man; For him light labour spread her wholesome store, Just gave what life required, but gave no more.

*— But times are altered ; trade's unfeeling train Usurp the land and dispossess the swain; Al n 8 the lawn, where scattered hamlets rose, Unwieldy wealth ard cumbrous pomp repose. A phase of recent social development in this country was forecast in this insurer :

Ye friends to truth, ye statesmen who survey The rich man's jny increase, the poor's decay ! "fis yours to judge how wide the limits stand Between a splendid and a happy land. Proud swells the tide with loads of freighted ore, And shouting folly hails them from the shore ; Hoards e'en beyond the miser's with abound, And rich men flock from all the world around. Yet count our gains ; this wealth is tut a name That leaves our useful products still the same. Not so our loss : The man of wealth and pride Takes up the spade that many poor supplied ; Space for his lake, his park's extended bounds, Space for his horses, equipage and hounds ; The robe that wraps his limbs in silken cloth Has robbed the neighbouring fields of half their growth; His seat where solitary spots are seen, Indignant spurns tho cottage from the green. Here appears the prototype of the modern tramp : Scourged by famine, from the smiting land The mournful peasant leads his humble band; And while he sinks, without one arm to save, The country blooms—a garden and a grave.

Even in Goldsmith's time the ancient commons were suffering invasion, to the detriment of the poor man, for— H to some common's fenceless limits strayed, He drives his flock to pick the scanty blade, Those fenceless fields the sons of wealth divide, And even the bare, worn common is denied.

The vision of a Henry George could not be more clear in detecting the deplorable condition of the land- less, growing out of the progress and poverty of the community. The poet wrote If to the city sped—what waits him there? To see profusion that he must not share; To see ten thousand baneful! arts combined To pamper luxury and thin mankind ; To see each joy the sons of pleasure know Extorted from his fellow creatures' woe. Here, while the courtier glitters in brocade, The pale artist plies his sickly trade; Here while the proud their long-drawn pomns display, There the black gibbet glooms beside the way. The dome where pleasure holds her midnight reign, Here richly decked, admits the gorgeous train; Tumultuous grandeur crowds the blazing square The rattling chariots dash, the torches glare. Sure, scene like these no troubles e'er annoy, Sure, 6c:se denote one universal joy! Are those thy serious thoughts? Ah, turn thine eyes Where the poor houseless shivering female lies; She once, perhaps, in village plenty blest, Has wept at tales of innocence distrest ; Her modest looks the cottage might adorn, Sweet as the primrose peeps beneath the thorn, Now lost to all ; her friends, her virtue fled, Near her betrayer's door she lays her head, And pinched with cold and shrinking from the shower, With heavy heart deplores that luckless hour When idly first, ambitious of the town, She left her wheel and robes of country brown. Then, as now, the young xvornen as well as the young men, were driven forth from the acres that ought to have supported them. Invoking the spirit of Truth, Goldsmith exclaimed : Teach man that states of native strength possest Though very poor may still be very blest; That trade's proud empire 'tastes to swift decay, As ocean sweeps the laboured mole away; While self-dependent power can time defy, As rocks resist the billows and the sky. Self dependent power in the people absolutely requires equal access to the bounties of nature, equal opportunities for the use of land. This is the lesson suggested by Goldsmith's inspired poem, and taught by all human experience. How can this equality of opportunity be brought about? By requiring all .to contribute equally to the community for exclusive privileges in the occupation and use of land. This is the foundation of all true philosophy. It is based on exact justice. The democracy is beginning to recognise this truth. In the beginning they determined to be free; that determination they have never relinquished. They are only begininnieg to perceive that freedom can come only by means of the Taxation of Land Values. When they see this,clearly, nothing can prevent its adoption.

"WHAT OF THE FINISHED YEAR?" THE REV. DR. MELLOWS STRIKING

REVIEW.

CRITICISM OF THE WHITE SLAVE ACT.

The Rev, Dr Stanley Mellor,preaching at Cairo-street Chapel on Sunday evening, took for his subject "What of the finished year ?" He touched upon those events of the past year which he regarded as fraught with the gravest and most far-reaching significance for the future. Dealing first with relatively external happen- ings, he recalled the day, toile early in the past year, when the announcement was made that China bad become a republic ; the oldest of Empires suddenly converted to the newest of States. In the dim and silent East there was going fore ard

A MARVELLOUS AWAKENING. Amongst those untold millions of people, whose his- tory stretched back into long ages in comparison with which the life of the civilized %A eat was but a span, amongst the men and women who had ever been mysterious and unknown to us there was a new spirit abroau. They seemed to have heard a call from the future, clEar enough to waken a slumbering world. V%hat it might all betoken, this marvellous waking from sleep, only the future could reveal ; no-one dare prophesy. Possibly its inner meaning would not be clear till the civilizations of the West were laid in ruins. In any case the year that was closing would stand in history as a significant moment in the story of the East, the hour of a great awaken- ing from which there must be born a new and mar- vellous civilization. Alluding to the war in the South Eastern corner of Europe, the preacher said it eves interesting because, on the whole, it was NOT ENGINEERED BY CAPITALISTIC INFLUENCE nor carried through by mere greed of gain and desire for acquisition. It was a war deter- mined really by deep-seated racial antipathies, by the longing for revenge, and by the wish to remove an alien element from the Western 1 world. The Turks were a conquering people, really a foreign invader. They entered Europe in the 14th century and the memory of that ancient invasion had rankled during all those centuries in the minds of the peoples who felt its effect most. We bad, as a result, witnessed the recent effort to drive the Turks, the invaders, back to their own place. What the final outcome of it all might be we did not yet know. Changes in the map of Europe were not made lightly, and might not be preserved easily, especially when the so-called great Powers, who occupied this unfortunate Continent, seemed, one and all, bent on aggrandisement and occupied with preparations for war. It was curious, in passing, to observe how the arma- ments of Europe kept increasing year by year. We never heard of any lessening, but always of increase, and it was strange how impassively, ns a rule, we accepted the awful waste of money and of energy continually going on in this game of war—more than £70,000,000 thrown away in England alone this year, to satisfy vanity and foolishness. Where would it end? Were Empire and nationality worth the price? Ile did not think so ; it might be better to feed the hungry and clothe the naked. It might also be wiser to cease talking of Christian civilization I Some day, he supposed, there would be a true Armageddon. One ray of hope, indeed, there was in this matter of war, the light of which shone even over this most recent piece of appalling business. The WORKERS OF TIIE WORLD seemed to be getting tired of international jealousy and suspicion, from which war was bred. In all the capitals of Europe, huge meetings of protest against the Balkan war were held, supported and attended by the proletariat. It looted as it the workers might even yet learn that the only war in which they ought to engage was against the materialistic civilization which kept them shaves. Certainly the past year had seen that feeling grow stronger amongst the toiling masses of Europe and, in that, he for one rejoiced. An INTERNATIONAL STRIKE AGAINST ARMAMENTS AND

would be worth seeing and -Worth trying. Turying to happenings in our own national life, Dr Mellor said he supposed he would lay himself open to severe criticism because he was not going to say a word about the efforts of our legislators yy hi( h, general opinion supposed, reflected the will and the feelings of the nation at large. Parliament and the party system and all connected with it might go on quite unaieturbed, producing cumbrous Insurance Acts and what not to the private satisfaction of social reformers and politicians, for none of those things really mattered to this miserable country of curs What did really matter was going on underneath and quite away from the noisome air of politics—so much se that no sane man could look at the discussions in Parliament or the speeches of politicians without feel- ing their utter unreality and lack of contact with real events. Hoaever, be felt constrained; before passing to truly important things, to make reference to one PITIFULLY DISCREDITABLE PIECE, or SO-CALLED LEGISLATION which had rejoiced the English Parliament this year—he meant the White Slave Traffic Bill. That Bill was introduced as an attack upon a recognized evil which infected society pretty generally. A great and worthy task—and yet the collective imagination of 600 odd presumably sane men got no further than the notion of flogging, an utterly barbarous method of puniali- nt. As if the lash would touch an evil like this one, the cause) of which lay embedded in tbe basis of our social system, which offered to women the alternative of shame or staavaticn, turned our young people into the world utterly ignorant of things they ought to know, and covered up with false shame and spurious modesty a knowledge of facts which was indispensable to right living. Oh, the frightful ineptitude of these people! However, let that pass: the iniquity of the sexual aspect of our social and individual life would not be mended, but only made worse, by flagging History, et us hope, would place this piece of legisla- tion on record as one of the most startling proofs of the degradation of present-day society. And now a word about the EVENTS OF TIIE YEAR THAT REALLY MATTERED. They knew, be thought, quite well what they were : the strike of the miners, the strike of the transport workers, the quite recent strike of North-Eastern rail- way men, and the countless other smaller strikss of the past twelve months. Those tlaiugs mattered row and for the future. Behind them, underneath them, ass to be discerned a new spirit, the suggestion of a new way. There was displayed in those outbreaks a sense of unity end of class cnsciouaness previously rare in this country. There was present also a feeling of solidarity hitherto almost unknown in England and supported, especially as by the London dockera, with marvellous courage et d self-sacrifice. There was, too, a power of comradeship full of signifi- cance. People who said untrue ',lino declared that the workers of the North Eastern Railway came out on strike in order to secure to themselves the RIGHT TO GET DRUNK. That, of course, was not the case: They felt that one of their members had been unjustly and unfairly treated and—a very rare occurrence in industrial life—the cause of one became definitely the cause of all, and in the person of one man suffering from,iejustice there was found the embodiment of a whole class,a great fraternity oppressed and denied tbe rights of life. The railway - strike had nothing to do with intemperance, hut every- thing with justice, a thing of vrhich we were likely to know more in the future than we knew now. He saw in the events to which he had referred the omens of a new era of industrial warfare. He saw the opening up of a new sphere of desire and demand before the workers, and because all this threatened oor ciy ilizi- tion, he rejoiced. The growing distrust of the political machine was very welcome. The spread of revolu- tiouary ideas and revolutionary propaganda was equally good. In the underworld of such ideas and movements the future was being shaped and moulded. No power in earth or in heaven could prevent the foal disruption of our present social system, and the value of the great events of the past year was surely that they assisted the wise man to realize that fact' and might even help him to throw in his lot with the forces which moved forward and not back. We left the dying year with a grave distrust of the order of life about us,

The recent case in which Thomas Outwin, commercial traveller, of Latchford Without, was fined for dangerous motoring at Blackpool, was brought to the notice of the House of Commons on Tuesday. Mr T Richardson (Lab. W itehaven), on behalf of Mr P Snowden (Lab., Blackburn), asked what action it was proposed to take in the care of a motorist who was tined £IO in Octoter last by the Blackpool magistrates for reckless driving, whereby a woman was killed. Mr Ellis Griffith : I have made inquiry, and find that Thomas Outs in was convicted by the Blackpool justices of driving a motor-car in a manner dangerous to the public, and was !bed £lO and disqualified for holding a licence for two years from the date of con- viction. The defendant had hcen five times previouslw convicted of driving motor-cars and motor cycles to the danger of the public, and five tines of other offences in connection with motor-cars. In the case now in ques- tion it appeared from the evidence that he drove down the Promenade at Blackpool at a speed of from 16 to 20 miles an hour at a time of night when the places of amusement were discharging their audiences, and the roadway was crowded with people. Having regard to all the circumstances the sentence appeared to me to be E 0 inadequate to the offence that 1 felt it my d, ty to consult the Director of Public Prosecution as to the desirability of taking further proceedings against Outwin ler the manslaughter of the woman who was killed by hs car. IL it, in view of the time that bad elapsed since the offence was committed and of the fact that it bud already been twice investigated by a c mil of law, I came, somewhat reluctantly, to the con- clusion that furthcr criminal proceedings would scarcely be jusi Med. Ido not think that there is any necessity, for lesiaiation to increase penalties. A person con- victed for the second time of dangerous driving can be sentenced to three months imprisonment without the option of a fine, sad in the event of his causing the death of any person he is, of course, liable to prosecu- tion for manslaughter hose powers, if administered with firmness and discretion, should, in my opinion, be sufficient for the protection of the public, and I have ' recently issued a circular to justices calling their atten- tion, among other things, to the importance of impos- ing an adequate punishrneet for serious offlnces, such as dangerous driving. The fine imposed on Outwin was in my judgment, an ultogetli,r inadequate penalty.

BLACKBURN CONSERVATIVES.

DIFFICULTIES Dlt TO TARIFF REFORNI.

A Blackburn correspondent writes :—The Blackburn Conservative party have not yet secured a second candi- date to stand with Mr W B Boyd-Carpenter at the next general election. Among many members of the Acsociation there is a feeling in favour of the selection of a cindidate with some close connection with the borough, but the httest declaration of Mr Bonar v bus made such a task additionally difficult. Some time ago there was talk about Mr. Herbert J Whiteley, who w.ts nominated before the last election, but withdrew for family reasons. He has a close tie with the town and its staple industry, but any hope there might have been of his standing has been dispelled by his latest utterance entirely dis- approving ot the pcsiftm taken up on Tariff "Reform" by the Conservative leader. Ile has just written objecting to "other people settling our fiscal policy for us," and urging that " if food taxes are to be mixed up with home Rule at the next election, without any definite referendum promise, there will be many loyal Unionists wh9 will lose heart and cease actively to support the Unionist cause." Mr Whitely proposes tbat the party should allow Tariff " Reforru " to stand on one side for the time bcing, and fight the next election on the Irish question and general politics. Several of Blackburn's leading Conservatives have their doubts on the fiscal question' and the local situation is growing more interesting each day.

IV.—THE GREAT INSURANCE ACT: But now I must go back again, this time to May, 1911, and the introduction of the great Insurance Act, the most splendid piece of sane, sound, solid Imperialism of our times. For this Act also is not without a very important .bearing on the political history of 1912. Now, why did the Govern- ment put its hand to the stupendous task which this Act represents? Certainly not to court popular favour. You do not go out for popular favour in this , country with compulsion in your hands. Does anyone suppose that Mr. Gladstone's Ministry of 1870 framed the Elementary Education Act of that year with a view to the cultivation of popularity? No! Grave as was the task, the truest conception of Imperialism, the truest re gard for national well-being, demanded the application of that disagreeable operation known as compulsion. To what end?, To compel all those seven or eight millions who make little or no provision to-day to do, by legal pressure, that which the other six millions, enrolled in the various thrift societies, have already done voluntarily. That is all. That is the Insurance Act in a nutshell.

Of . course, the authors of this great scheme knew that there would be grum- bling, -misconception, irritation at first. They knew that the touch of compulsion would be resented until by rapid transmu- tation the duty it enforced merged into an acquired habit, which it ,will do rapidly. They knew all that. But the thing had to be done. And they did it.

sions! Then there is £1,500,000 in the 1911-12 Budget. for sanatoria. There is another £3,000,000 for the Insurance Act in 1912-13. There will be a further £6,300,000 for that Act in 1913-14. Never in the history of our country has such bounteous provision been made for our people. And before I leave this splendid en- deavour to ensure the peace of mind and health of body of the workman let me say this. Already it has accomplished the most far - reaching social reform that the most ardent of us dared imagine. It has more than doubled the number of those ready for the rainy day. No doubt included in the great. army of workpeople which this statement em- braces there is a narrow fringe of the mis- cellaneous camp followers of organised in- dustrialism the casuals particularly— who will not find it easy to pick up the new step forward. We have endeavoured to meet their case by a• scale of reduced con- tributions for workpeople at very low rates of wages. But even then the application of the scheme to the poorest members of the community will need the closest and most sympathetic watching. We are already in- vestigating the case of the outworker. We have undertaken to review the whole case of the Post Office depositor in, three years' time. For the rest, it is our duty—it is our duty as the authors of this tremendous 'en- deavour—to watch the working of the scheme day by day. It is our duty to come forward the moment practical experience may indicate the necessity, and submit whatever proposals .may seem expedient for the more easy adjustment of the obliga- tions which the Act imposes upon our poorer brethren. VIII.—FOUR-SQUARE TO EVERY WIND. It has been impossible for me, in the space at my disposal, even to mention many of the more important political incidents of the year—the injury done to the cause of Woman's Suffrage by the criminal folly of a section of the supporters of that move- ment; the patient, statesmanlike hand- ling of the miners' strike by the Prime Minister; the spirit of consent with which the great mass of our fellow-countrymen sees the granting of Home Rule to Ireland approach consummation. But I cannot close even this incomplete record without a grateful reference to the proceedings in the Canadian House of Commons on December sth. It would be impertinent on my part to anticipate the further discussion of Mr. Borden's Navy Bill ; bet the spirit which has inspired that bill—a spirit which finds sympathetic response in all parts of the Empire—is of the deepest significance. It is a splendid witness to the recognition by the Oversea Dominions of their vital stake in the in- tegrity of the Empire. As I think, it gives the happiest assurance to all of us, here and across the seas, of the continued pros- perity, consolidation, and unity of the British peoples. all the world over. Their aim and purpose are simple. They .harbour no against any other people. They seek quietly and peaceably to go their way, developing their institu- tions, solving their social problems, strengthening and perfecting the founda- tions of liberty and good government of which the genius of their forebears has made them the heritors. These great ends cannot be pursued save under cover of ade- quate defence. The fact is a cynical com- ment, if you like, upon the condition of the world in this the year of grace 1912. But there it is; and it has got to be faced. Further, the cost of this vital adequate defence is very heavy, and is growing. That each part of the British Empir?, should be found ready to play its part in shouldering the burden is the true memo- rial to the sagacity and statesmanship of those who laid the broad lines upon which our great Empire has been built.

'OLITICS IN 1912,

13Y RT. lION. T. J. 'MACNAMARA, M.P.

(Secre!ary to the Admiralty).

1.-LOOKING BACK.

If you are to read the political history of 1912 with full understanding, you must go back nine years. You must go back to 1903 and the starting of the great Tariff Reform campaign. It seemed incredible that the people of these islands---people vitally de- pendent for their food and occupation upon cheap duty-free importations of food and raw material, people whose Industry and Commerce have so bounteously expanded under a Free-Trade system—should find themselves confronted with a proposal to go back to Protection. Yet that invitation was boldly pressed upon them—pressed upon them with relentless determination and consummate skill. Most if not all of

our industrial shortcomings were due to

Free Trade. There was not one of them that would not yield to the treatment of this sovereign cataplasm. To say that the age of miracles had passed was nonsense. Unemployment, rural depopulation, labour unrest, pauperism, low wages—each and all were, if not entirely to be removed, at any rate to be sensibly affected by this rare and magic solvent. Thus this astonishing campaign pro- ceeded till we came to the dark days of the industrial depression of 1908-9. Unem-i ployment had risen to alarming propor- tions; there was much distress; there was much misery. All were promptly attri- buted to Free Trade. Tariff Reform seduc- tively pointed the way to better times. This was the critical moment. Through the gloom of that time the assurances of

the Tariff Reformer glittered attractively.

And, to make matters worse for Free Trade, the Chancellor of the Exchequer had to find fourteen , new millions of tax revenue. It was now or never with Tariff Reform, as Mr: Chamberlain shrewdly saw. Give the Tariff Reformer his chance, and soon the outlook would assume a far rosier complexion. He would raise your new revenue for you with the greatest ease. The foreigner should pay. No .conjurer could get you young rdbbits out of a silk hat with

less trouble. Your loaf would be cheaper. Thera should be no increase in the cost of

living; no increase in the proportion of taxation borne by the working man. On the contrary. So far from these hardships fall- ing upon him, there should be More Work and Higher Wages for him.

lI.—TORYISM'S CLASSIC BLUNDER. And what was the Radi,ml and Free Trade alternative? Nothing iLashy, nothing mysterious, nothing miraculous. Simply a bold step forward along the lines of demo- cratic finance mar.ked out by previous Radical and Free-Dade Chancellors of the Exchequer. Mr. Lloyd George surveyed the situation. The cost of government was increasing, and seemed likely to continue to increase. Existimg taxes were not like the widow's cruse of oil. You could not keep on tapping them and expect a richer har- vest every time. Bes'cles, -your system was not altogether equitaixle. Here the taxes pressed too heavily; Caere too lightly. The thing wanted overhauling. It wanted demo- cratic revision. And dem. wratic revision it got!

Well,. there you are! There were the two alternatives. And so ;ocksure was Toryism that hard times had 1:1:led Free Trade that it perpetrated the classic peditical blunder alrf the age. It counselled reiection of the Budget. Very good. We wend; to the country. And to .Toryism's eternal amazement we came back victorioWs. Natu rally to their noble Lordships we said: Never again! Never again will you touch f_riame. We will put in black on white wlui.t. had been the recog- nised and estalAished rule .of the Constitu- tion for centuries. Farther, in the field of general legislation we will make you con- cede to Radical proposals tnot indeed the obsequious acquiesce: ace you have always conceded to Tory proposals, but simply fair play. So we framed and: passed. our Veto reso- lutions, and issue& our Parliament Bill. We went to a sec 31x1. General Election in December, 1910, a dtd ngain came back vic- tors. TORYMM IS ANGRY.

Now, I have vehearsee_ this story because 'without it ycari cannot accuxately read. the political hislary- of 1912. V.-TORYISM AND THE ACT.

But now consider the attitude of the Tory Party generally towards the Act. I say generally, because there were noble ex- ceptions. Confronted with this great and patriotic scheme, I confess I should have expected to hear Toryism talking some- thing .like th's :

"Well! Heaven knows there's a great deal of misery in our midet ! And certainly something must be done. This scheme isn't perfect; but nothing human ever is. Still, with its sickness benefit, its medical benefit, its sanatorium bene- fit, and, last but by no moans least, its thirty shillings' welcome for the new baby, this thing will be a perfect God- send for the poor people. That being so, let's do the patriotic thing over this, at any rate. Let's lay aside for once- the party policy of simply looking at your opponent's proposals to see how best they may be denounced Let's lend a hand." In point of fact, that is the attitude Toryism took up at the outset. But it was too good to last. Steadily the glass began to fall, and the more you tapped it the more it backed. Why? The thing was 60 startling in its compulsion, so sweeping in its inclusion, and so complex in the details of its application, that all sorts an con- ditions of men and women began to get fidgety and apprehensive. Small em- ployers of labour did not quite see why. Many very poor people—those on the fringe of the industrial army—really could not see how.

It was then, as I think, Toryism. fell. By way of apology it tells you that its hostility was due to the curtailment of discuesion.in the House of Commons: ,Nonsense. That is a clumsy afterthought. There was no curtail- ment of discussion down to the autumn session of 1911. Ask- the Radical candi- dates at the by-elections just preceding- the opening of that, session. They will tell you how very cordially disposed Toryism was to the Act. However, we -are now rapidly 'nearing the close of the "waiting period, and soon the Insurance Act will cease to be the "vote winner" for Toryism at the by- elections.

VI.-TORYISM'S SECOND CLASSIC

BLUNDER.

Which' brings me to November 14th, the Albert Hall, and the momentous declara- tions of policy on behalf of the Unionist Party by Lord Lansdowne and Mr. Bonar Law. Again Toryism is cocksure--as cock- sure as in November, 1909. Far more so, be it observed, than it was on the eve of the General Election of December, 1910. Then it told the people that there should be no Tariff Reform until the matter had been specifically referred to the electorate by way of Referendum. Clearly it then thought that victory might be snatched by this " Stop-Press " announcement. The ex- pedient failed. So Toryism has no further use for the Referendum on Tariff Reform. It tells all whom it may concern, quite frankly, that if it wins the next General Election come that when it may it will go straight for Tariff Reform. It tells you more. In pursuance of its policy of Colonial Preference, it will hold itself free —if the Dominions ask for it—to clap "a moderate duty on foreign wheat." It must be cocksure. Just as cocksure as it was in November, 1909.

So let us see exactly where we stand. Toryism's view clearly is this--that the temporary unpopularity oi the Insurance Act—which unpopularity is bound steadily to disappear as the calendar for. 1913 is ticked off—should enable it, if it can strike soon, to lead its forces to victory. And what then?

1. The Parliament Act is to be revoked. 2. A tax may be placed on Toreign corn. 3. Free Trade is to he undermined.

Further, so far as the rank and file of :the Tory Party is concerned—if lam to judge from the speeches at the National Unionist Conference at the Queen's Hall on this historic November 14th, 1912—there are a good many of them who would add a fourth item, viz., the setting up of some form of compulsory military training. I headed all this, " Toryism's Second Classic Blunder." For I am profoundly convinced that Toryism can no more win on this, come the struggle when it may, than could. Burgess have swum the Channel with a mill-stone about his neck.

===== 0002601_19130104_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
VII.-A VERY DIFFERENT STORY.

I turn from this hotch-potch of impossi- bilities to the simple, straightforward Fis- cal policy of Radical, Free Trade, demo- eratic finance. Note its results. Under the Lloyd George Budget—that cordially de- tested instrument—a boundless income has been secured, and secured justly, equitably, and without undue hardship upon any class in. the community. From each 'ac- cording to his capacity, to each according to his needs—that is its purpose and pro- vision. With the magnificent means it has placed at the Government's disposal, ample provision has been made for the cost of de- fence against the possibility of successful attack from without. And more. Provision has been made, .the like of which has never been attempted in our history, to take se- curity that we do not fall from the ravages of decay from within.

And let this be observed. Radicalism has entered into great and unprecedented com- mitments on.behalf of the working people. Free Trade Democratic Finance has made that possible—and made it possible side by side with actual reductions in the taxa- tion which presses especially upon the poorer people. In 1906-7 the Government reduced the tea tax from 6d. to sd. in the pound. In 1908-9 it reduced the sugar tax from 4s. 2d. to is. 10d. per cwt. The one meant a relief of something like £1,120,000 a-year, the other a relief of something like £3,400,000 a-year. Further, the National Debt has been reduced by £64,000,000 since the present Government took command. Here are "bettec times" for the poorer folk if you like—reduced taxation followed b 5; £12,000,000 a-year for Old-Age Pen.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

Twelfth Day.—As a popular festival, Twelfth Day used t ) be second only to Christmas, which is not so very surprising when we remember that it was old Christmas Day. The occa-eon was associated with eating, drinking, and dancing, but it is rarely kept as a festival nowadays, although the religious celebra- tion of the Feast of the Epiphany is more generally i observed than it used to be. It s different on the C mtinent, and in Tentanic countries the day of the "drei Konegen," is the all-important One in the matter of giving presents. Modern economic conditions will not permit of the twelve days merry-making of the old- fashioned Christmas, and when January 6th arrivas, even the most festive spirits have had enough of the secular part of the celebration which comes but once a year. Those people who indulge in Twelfth Night parties, and a twelfth cake, should remember that Christmas decorations ought to In out of the house beforehand on pain of ill-luck through the coming year. The Hostess and Her Guests.—When making lip a house party for Christmas or the New Year,the hostess cannot always pick and choose her guests, as she can at other seasons. Tuere are some frienda or relatives who must be asked, although known to be of a trying type. There is, for instance, the visitor, as often a roan as a woman, who arrives with an ailment, and reriaires special dietary, tire, hot-water bottles ; who objeAs to an open window, or cannot do this or that, and for whom there is only one possible bedroom—the one that can least be spared. You not only feel thankful when this visitor departs, you also feel years older. Then there is the visitor who wants to ba entertained, and can do nothing for himielf. He is full of likes and dislikes, and you hive to lock after him incessantly, or else he will impart his boredom and discomfort to others and spoil your party. Anotiter guest who gives a great deal of troable to the erratic parson who arrives a day or two late, aid has sent on his luggage which has not arrived. Ile is late for breakfast, uncertain in turning up for meals, and will insist on your listening to oreary anecdotes late nit night, long after you want to retire. Worst of all perhaps, is the visitor who comes for a week and stays for months. You do not have to work yourself to death for this person, it only becomes wearying and monotonous. Of another type is the ideal guest, who never gives unnecessrry trouble, who is helpful and enthusiastic, in entertaining others, who never takes you from your household duties, but is always at hand to help and to talk when you have leisure. If one is fortunate in having such visitors, it makes it easier to put up with the rest.

The Pantomime.—Pantomime has been said, by a French writer, to date back to a period just after Noah had quitted the Ark, that, no doubt, was a passing criticism on John Bull and his ways, for the pantomime is, and always has been, almost exclusively a British institution. The first pantomime which described itself as such. entitled " Tho Tavern Bilkers, " was produced at the Drury-lane Theatre in London, in 1702, but the complete performance on modern lines does not date back more than a hundred years. The Victorian panto- mimes were very different from the gorgeous spectacle now produced, for which one or other of some half- dozen fairy tales furnishes the title. Taking one yelr with another, " Cinderella " is the favourite theme as a foundation for the pantomime, as it is this season. Possibly one of the reasons for its popularity, is that it has a moral, and the story figures in no fewer than 21 of the pantomimes produced this season, six of them being in London and the suburbs. Not that it matters whether Blue Beard, Dick Whittington, Aladdin, or Cinderella provides the story. The success of the pantomime depends on two things : its scenic effects, and the crowding of "the book " with topical skits concerning the chief events of the year, political and otherwise.

A Minimum Wage Scale for Women and Girls.—The present year is destined to witness a new extend i -m of the minimum wage movement, applying to some 11,000 women and girl workers employed by co-operative societies. A minimum scale, beginning at 51 a week at fourteen years of age, and rising 2s a year to 173 at I twenty, is to be adopted. The movement to secure better conditions for co-operative female employes was commenced by the Women's Co-operative Guild some four years ago, and the minimum scale has been adopted by 180 distributing societies, and also for the 7,000 women and girls employed by the wholesale societies. If these conditions are possible in industrial concerns under working-class management, they should be possible elsewhere, and they will help to stimulate the demand for a mitional minimum wage of 15s. a week for women workers. A decent " living wage " is one of the first essentials to the prevention of social evils. There may he no eisy means for making model employers by Act of Parliament, but women and girls form the majority of the widespread victims of the sweated industries, and if effective measures could be taken to help this struggling mass of humanity, much would Le done to help forward social reform. Widow-Brides.—Once upon a time the toilettes of widow-brides were almost invariably composed of cold hard, grey tints—which all too frequently proved very unbecoming. But we have changed all that, and now-a-days the widow-bride dons any hue becoming to her. Quite recently, a very handsome wide) N-bride wore a gold colour,another palest blue, and yet another maize with sable stole and muff. The woman past her youth declines to turn herself into a dismal, dowdy, and most uninteresting object, as was the custom up to a decade or so ago, and the change is decidedly, and from every point of view a desirable one. The Tea-Frock.—When the tea gown made its first appearance, it was distinguished for simplicity and comfort, and met a distinct want. Nor was it very expensive, or too elaborate for the craft of the borne- dressmaker, who possessed some skill with her needle. But all this is altered now. The one time modest tea- gown, has developed into a gorgeous garment, quite out of place at s) informal a meal as a "five o'clock." It has become also a very costly affair and suited only to theatre or evening wear. But a loose, warm, and comfortable dress, that can take the place of the tea- gown is an absolute necessity for society women, and others also. Thus the tea-frock, a glorifiad afternoon frock Las come into being and as it can be easily donned and doffed, is in comparison simple and quite elegant, its success is assured. It can be worn as an afternoon frock as well as for tea, and thus answers a double purpose, another factor in its favour. 'The Tunic Style and the Tailor Made.—Many velvet and velveteen tailored suits have a novel and very practical feature, in which the tunic idea is exploited to great advantage. The suit is in two pieces, a cult and a dress. The latter is in princess style, the upper part to the knees being of wool-back satin-broche, or silk, the same colour, or a contrast, according to taste. 'rho lower part of the skirt is of velvet to match the coat, and when worn together the effect is that of a coat and skirt and blouse. By this means, the vogue for mixed materials in tailored as in other dresses is successfully carried out.

The Blouse that Fastens in Front.—Basques are added to nearly all evening blouses. Sometime these are deep sometimes very narrow with rounded corners. Often the bssque consists of a frill of lace, even when the blouse is made of silk, or some other material. The Mag7tar blouse is by no means defunct. Most women find the back fastening of a blouse inconvenient, there- fore will welcome the news that both for day and even- ing wear these useful and becomirg garments are now fastened in front, and that they are likely to remain in fashion throughout he season. Slanting fastenings are sometimes seen, as a change from straight. Quite a novelty in blouses is a Magyar Isith long sleeves fastened in front, with high collar and deep basques. This style resembles a loose coat somewhat.

CHURCH AND STAGE.

Bishop Welldon's Plea for Pure Performances.

Bishop \Tendon, Dem of Manchester, preaching to the n;embers of the theatrical profession at Manchester Cathedral, on Sunday, said that a sympathetic under- standing between the Church and stage might be honourable and valuable if it were based upon a clear recognition of their several offices and functions. No sensible person could believe that the Church and stage sought the same objects. The Church aimed at converting the souls of men fighting against sin, and promoting personal and [oda! righte Jusness by extending the divine Kingdom on earth. That was not and could not be the object hich the theatrical people set themselves. Their part was to 8111t160, to instruct, and to refine the minds of men: to provide them with healthy, agreeable enter- tainment in the intervals of their daily toil, cultivating their tastes and dispositions ; to make their common life n little happier by taking them now and again for a while out of the hard, prosaic, actual world which they know so well into a world of light, of fancy, and of imagination. The members of the theatrical pro- fession would make as grave a mistake if they were to be preach(rs on the stage as he would if he were to be an hctor in the pulpit. The relations between them might be very useful, because they were different from another. There was no truer test of a nation's moral status than the sort of amusement in which it took pleasure. The Church, no less than the stage, depended in the last resort upon the essential and immutable facts of human nature. While it was in the power of the stage to broaden the sympathy of the Church, no less was it in the power of the Church to elevate the associations of the stage. Bishop Welldon pleaded with them to keep their performinces in the theatre and music hall as clear as possible from every taint and stain of immorality. Let them realise that the Church and stage had interests and obligations in common; let them join in seeking the good of human nature, and maybe they would become better friends.

THE WHITE SLAVE TRAFFIC CRUSADE.

We ask for greater supervision of vessels taking out emigrants to our Colonies and to foreign countries, and that whenever young wo.nen are taken as passengers there shall ha suitable femile superintendence on hoard and provision for safe temporary lodgment at the port of destination, unless this has been satisfactorily pro. vided for by friends of the travellers. We hive reason to think that there is great indifference, not to say wilful blindness, as to conduct on board ships, and as to the facilities allowed to those who promote the evils that we fight against on shore. We shall not be content with stopping the traffic, only in Great Britain. We are responiible for the conduct of her Depeni- encies, and ought not to forget the girls, both European and Asiastie, who are practically enslaved in India, Singapore, Hong Kong,Egypt and elsewhere Perhaps what is most impressive of all has been the responseof the country to the appeal, and it is this that raises our hopes. If the country resolves that the great evil of prostitution shall have no quarter, the question will ba studied from all p Ants. The sanctity of marriage will be insisted upon, and especi- ally will effort be made to prevent the many thought- less, loveless unions which go by the name of marriage. The training of our children in the knowledge of the functions of life and thair sacred import will be realised as one of our highest duties. The economic question will be studied with the view of just wages being paid to women for their work; better conditions of labour must also be insisted upon and the moral protection of the worker.—Lady Bunting in the January' Contemporary Review."

TRADING AND FAILURES.

Our trading prosperity during the past twelve months fully reflected in the figures for the year's failures. Taking the grand totals for the whole of the United Kingdom,we find that the official failures in bankruptcy and by deeds of arrangement come out at only 7,874. This (says " Kernp's Mercantile Gazette") is by far the lowest recorded since 1888, the first year under the existing system of deeds for arranging with creditors. In glancing down the columns, we see totals of over 10,000, and more than 9,000, and it was only in 1911 that we got lower, to 8,351, which, during 1912, fell to 7,874, showing a remarkable total decrease of 480. Turning to the separate tables for England ana Wales, we find that bankruptcies declined from 4,461 in 1911 to 4,253 in 1912, and deeds of arrangement from 3,893 to 3,621, showing in the result a decrease of 208 bankruptcies, and 272 deeds of arrangement, the fall-off in the latter class being thus in a much highec rate of proportion, which seems, perhaps, rather surprising. These statistics are certainly satisfactory, as evidence of the general prosperity of trade during the period they cover. Trade, and more especially shipping, has had a great boom, the explanation of which is not easily discovered. For the whole world has been doing well and making money, countries most widely separated in their political and economic systems and business methods, being equally prosperous. Amongst. ourselves, very few trades have done badly. Even, builders and the timber trade are better than they have, been of late years, their total decreases in official 4 failures coming out as high as 119. The drapery trades, also, sho* a good decrease, as also the jewellery and fancy trades, results which are clearly due to the general trade prosperity bringing more money about for spending purposes on things outside the necessaries of life.

NEW YEAR'S HONOURS.

The New Year's Honours List published on Wednes- day morning includes two new peers, four privy councillors, seven baronets, and eighteen knights. One of the new peers is Sir George Kemp, of Rochdale, formerly Liberal member for North-west Manchester. The other is Sir George Sydenham Clarke, a distinguished military authority, who has recently retied from the Governorship of Bombay. Both peerages are baronies of the United Kingdom. Sir John Simon, the Solicitor General, is among the new privy councillors. Mr Thomas Graham Jackson, R.A., Dr Francis Darwin, and Vice•Adiniral Sir Percy Scott are among the new baronets, and Lieutenant Colonel Yorke, the chief inspecting officer of railways for the Board of Trade, and Mr Stewart Stockman, the chief veterinary officer of the Board of Agriculture, and Mr Frank Ree, the general manager of the London and North-wesiern Railway Company among the knights. Lieutenant Colonel J Fitzroy Bagot, the member for the Kendal division, is also among the baronets, and Mr Richard Walter Essex, the member for Stafford, and Mr. T. Benjamin Bowring, the senior Londn director of a welt-known Liverpool firm of merchants nd ship-owners, among the knights.

"IF I WERE A MILLIONAIRE." Great thoughts for January (Smith's Publishing Co. 61) is the double Christmas number, together with the popular sheet almanack, containing a helpful thought for every day in the year,dates of notable anniversaries, &c. The literary contents are remarkably interesting. In the course of an interview, Mr Norman Angell, the author of " The Great Illusion," is asked, " Has this present war changed your old opinions ?" " No," replied Mr Angell: " Certainly not ; why should it ? Surely we are not to say that because war has broken out that fact disposes of all objection to it. I suppose cannibalism and infanticide polygamy, judicial torture religious persecution, witchcraft, during all the years we did these" inevitable" things, were defended in the same way, and those who resented all critictsm of them pointed in triumph to the cannibal feast, the dead cAid, the maimed niLness, the slain heretic, or the burned witch. But the fact did not prove the wisdom of those habits, still less their inevitability ; for we have them no more." Among many interesting articles is one by Mr G W E Russell, on "If I were a millionaire." The famous Churchman says : "Whenever the opportunity served I should buy slum property in big towns, make a clean sweep of insanitary areas, add secure open spaces for public gardens and playgrounds for children. If this process involved the destruction of workmen's houses, I should be much inclined to contribute to "Garden Cities," taking pains to secure that only a" living rent" should be demanded. I should leave Public Libraries to Mr Carnegie, and a Nationll Theatre to those who believe in it, and Punch and Judy to the rates. To hospitals I should contribute on the largest scale, always taking uncommonly good care that any tendency on the part of doctors to experiment on the patients should be carefully watched, and not a penny of my money was bestowed on scientific torture. All forms of physical exeicise and outdoor recreation I should encourage by stibstantial prizes, doing all I could to enforce the distinction between—on the one hand—the games which discipline the human body; and—on the other—the brutal sports which torture dumb animals, and the systems of military training which would teach boys to;be murderers before their time.'

Ebenezer Church, Buttermarket-street. On New Year's Day a Band of Hope tea party and concert were held in the above church, and was well attended by members and friends. At the concert the chair was taken by Mr H Thornton. The programme included recitations by Lilian Shaw, Mrs Taylor, Miss Daisy Boughey, Miss Annie Boughey, Elwyn Reece and George Hazior. An action song was given by the children entitled "The Christmas Stocking." Solos were rendered by Miss Sarah Fox, Mrs Behbington and Mr Walter Ward, A duet was admirobly given by Miss Emily Higham and Miss Alice Gough. Songs were given by Miss Annie Boughey end Miss Minnie Shaw. There was also a dialogue given entitled '" The New Kitchenmaid,"

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FIFTY YEARS ON THE RAILWAY. Mr Taylor, stationmaster at Widnes, London and North-Western Railway station, is itb.,ut to retire after completing half a century's service, having risen from the position of a platelayer. Before his promotion to stationmaster Mr Taylor was for many years inspector of signalmen for the Liverpool and Warrington districts. WIDNES RAILWAY SUSPENSION. A flutter of excitement was caused in railway circles on Monday by the suspension from duty of Mr Henry Ackerley, a Socialist., who is employed as a singleman on the London and North-Western Railway at Widnes. As recently as Saturday he was defeated in a municipal by-election in Halton Ward. BEDROOM STRUGGLE.

SCENE IN A WIDNES HOTEL. At Widne., on Monday, Robert Atkinson, said to be a native of London, was charged with breaking and entering the Commercsal Hotel, Ann-street East.— Superintendent Foster stated that the prisoner was "en by the licensee of the hotel, Mr R Hill, partly concealed in a bedroom. Mr Hill heard the breaking of glass whilst he was in the kitchen, and whom going to investigate saw the prisoner as stated. A struggle ensued, and 'Mr Hill threw prisoner on to a bed and called for help. In the struggle, the prisoner bit the licensee. The police were communicated with, and Atkinson was apprehended. It was found that an entrance had been effected by scaling a wall and getting on to a ledge near to the bathroom window.—Elizabeth Humphries deposed that while engaged holding prisoner down when her brother-in-law called tor assistance, prisoner bit her.— Prisoner was committed for trial at the Liverpool Quarter Sessions. RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS. The fortnightly meeting of the Runcorn Board of Guardians was held at Dutton on Tuesday, Mr J Spruce presiding.

TUBERCrLOSIS REGULATIONS. The Local Government Board had forwarded a letter With regard to tuberculosis regulations. The Clerk Pointed out that the regulations set out that the Medical Officer for the Workhouse and the District Medical Officer would hay to wake reports from time to time to the Medical Offi3er of Health and the fee allowed was Is. for each report. Toe relieving officers would not be required to make notifications of tuberculosis patients. POOR LAW CONFERENCE. Notice was produced of the Central Poor Law C.ln- ference to be held at the Guild Hall, London on the 11th and 12th February, 1913. Mr Woodward gave notice that at the next meeting he would wove the appointment of delegates.

LIGTHING OF THE COTTAGE HOMES. With reference to the lighting of tae Cotiage Homes the Clerk said that they would remember that at a previous meeting they acoeptod the tender of £ll5 046 d for a single engine and the necesss.ry accumulators and accessories. They also instructed the architect to obtain a tender for a duplicate engine. Messrs Smith had sent one in for £3O lOs and the architect said that it was a reasonable price. The tender was accepted. GUARDIANS TO ADVERTISE FJR TENDERS. !. The Clerk read the minutes of the Coildren's Home Sub-Committee meeting held that morning. The Com- mittee recommended that Messrs Spruce, Pilling and ;the Master be empowered to purchase the furniture ,required and that the same gentleman be authorised to visit firms who specialised in furniture for children's quarters. Tenders for 50 bedsteads were to he obtained [from Messrs Monks, Hall and Co., Ltd., Warringlan [OM from a Birmingham firm. The bedding was to be ;tbtainld fr7Ol nerrs lte au Clarke, Warringtma and 11Ir Wright, of Halton. Mr Wood said he wonld like the recoalmendation to ibe altered slightly. He referred to the last portion 'which gave the order for bedding either to Mr Le-, of 1 Warrington, or Mr Wright, of Halton. He thought it was unfair to limit such a large order to two firms. There were drapers in Runcorn who would be in a Position to tender if tenders were advertised tor. They paid rates arid ought to have a chance. It was setting .a most unusual precedent aid he thought that. seeing • that the amlunt spent would be about .£lOO, it would be better to advertise for tenders He moved that as an amendment to the recommendations. The tihairman put the minutes of the sub-committee ' before the meeting end announced that he .thought the recommend itions of the sub-committee were approved. Mr Wood: Ido not think so. Let there be a show of hands. ' This course was adopted and six voted for the amendment and six against. The Chairman gave his casting vote in favour of Mr Wood's amendment and tenders for bedding will be advertized for.

LYMM. NEW YEAR'S DANCE.—On New Year's Eve another of the popular dances organized by Messrs Clare and Holt was held in the Drill Hall. The good attendance showed once again that dancing is a pleasure which "age doth not wither nor custom stale," fhe music, which consisted of all the latest pieces was provided by Mr Tonk's band. All the persons present joined in dancing the Old Year out and the New Year in to the strains of " Horne Sweet Home." WELCOMING THE NEW YEAR.—After a lapse of several years the Lymm Subscription Bend revived the old custom of playing at the Cross for public dancing during the mystic hour which surrounds the passing away of the Old Year and the advent of the New. The Parish Church bells were also rung from 11.30 p.m. on December 31st, and at the hour of midnight the tolling of the single bell was changed to a full and joyous peal which to the accompaniment of the factory, boat and engine whistles heralded the birth of the year 1913 ; in fact, so numerous were the various sounds which came across the still air that for three-quarters of an hour one might have thought that the relief of Ladysmith or , other celebrations were being observed. CHRISTMAS TREAT.—The scholars of the Sundax Schools connected with the Oughtrington St Peter'S Church, (Sandy-lane, Rush Green', tiroomedge, and Higher-lane) held their Christmas teas and prize distributions on Monday and Tuesday last. The bigger childreu held their gathering on the former day when a concert provided by scholars from each of the schools was given. On the following day the infants participated in the arrangements that had been made tor their enjoyment which included that source of joy to many a cnild's heart—a Christmas Tree. The Vicar, the Rev E P Wethered, M.A., found willing assistance from such workers as the Mesdames Blease and Parry, the Misses Brereton, Bolt, Brazendale, M Burrows, Forster, Telfer, B Timperley and M Timperley, and Mr FREE CHURCH WATCHNIGHT SERVICE.--The Free Church Council solemnized the passing of the old and advent of the New Year by holding a Watch-night service in the Wesleyan Church. Mr J D B Smith, the hon. secretary, took charge of the meeting, which was of a most helpful character. As the basis of his remarks , he chose Revelations xxi„ 22v., from which he gave a splendid address. He made special reference to the churches of Lymm, and spoke of the great and good work that might be accomplished if one and all were animated by a single desire for the spiritual betterment iof the village, and worked more in unison for the attainment of that object. PRIMITIVE METHoDIST SUNDAY SCHOOL.—Follow- ing upon the suscesst al public tea and concert held on Boxing Day, the scholars of the Primitive Methodist Sunday schcol celebrated their annual Christmas treats. Friday evening we.s devoted to the elder scholars, who, after doing themselves credit at the tea tables spent an enjoyable evening in playing various indoor games. 10 addition to the usual form of amuse- ments there was introduced a new feature under the title of "Chinese Christmas Puddings" which gave rite to much laughter, especially so when such articles as a dummy and sedlitz powder were brought to light. There was a full attendance of the junior school on Saturday night, the members of which had a happy time at both the tea and games. The little ones were full of life and fun, Si) much so that the elders in charge round their position no sinecure. The usual Christmas free was relegated to the hack ground on the present occasion and a bran tub with its store of surprises greatly added to the attractions of a jolly time. Prizes which had not been distributed on fhursday evening were presented to Frank Cheetham,Margery Priestner,and John Priestner. One was pleased to note the interestgvvhich the minister and all the school officials took in the gatherings. Owing to the generosity of Mr and Mrs H Champ the scholars of the broom Bdge Primitive Methodist Sun- day School indulged in a Christmas Party on Thursday evening. URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

- --- The monthly meeting of the Lymm UrbAn Distrct Council was held in the Council Chamb2r on Monday, Mr W G Groves presiding.
