LAND NOT TO BE ENCLOSED. At a meeting of the Roads Committee on December 10th a letter was read from Mr C Healey asking for permission to enclose the lend in front Of the shop ut Rush Green owned by the executors of the late Mr T Marsh, by erecting an iron palling. The committee declined to allow the land to be enclosed.

MOTOR CAUTION NOTICES. It was also resolved at a meeting of this committee that applic-ition be made to the Couaty Council asking them to erect two motor caution notices at the approach to Statham School. DAMAGE TO LAMPS. At a meeting of the Gas Committee the clerk wEs instructed to issue placards offering a reward of 105. for information, and afterwards attending before the magistrates to enable a conviction to be outained against persons damaging lamps. THE DISPUTED GAS ACCOUNT. At a meeting of the same Committee on December 19th,letters were read from Mr Thorp and Mr Finch, on behalf of the Wesleyan chapel. It was resolved that the clerk write to them " that the Council could not add anything fi.rther to what they had already stated and to again ask for payment of account." PLANS APPROVED FOR JOLLY THRASHER." At a meeting of the Building and Fire Engine Com- mittee a plan of the proposed additions and alterations at the" Jolly Thrasher " Inn for Messrs Elydes, Queen's Ferry, was submitted and recommended to the Council for approval. DISINFECTOR AND WASIIHOUSE. At a meeting of the Sanitary Works Committee held on December 19th a letter was reef': from the Local Government Board re the proposed disinfector and washhouse at the hospital. An amended plan from the Local Government Board having been sunniitted, it was decided that it be a recommendation to the Council for approval. The minutes of the variou3 committees were approved HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. The Medical Otlieer'3 quarterly report which was adopted,showedthat estimating the population of Lymm at 5,010, the births regist3red totalled 7.14 males and 13 females. They had a birth rate of 21-5 per 1,000 per annum. The deaths were 15.13 registered in the dis- trict and two from outside districts, giving a net death rate for Lymm of 11.6 per 1,000 per annum. The infantile mortality Wits represented by three deaths out of 27 births registered. There had been 216 cases of infectious diseases, 215 of measles and one of chicken- pox. Two only died during the year. There was only one person who had died over the age of 65, and that was a wonderful thing for Lymm. THE CHAIRMAN'S THANKS. The Chairman thanked the Council for the kind resolution of s“npathy and condolence forwarded to him by the Clerk, on tie occasion of the death of his mother.

A DANGEROUS BRIDGE. Mr Holt referred to the dangerous state of Ditchfleld Bridge owing to the narrownek.s of the road. Ile said that 12 months ago he drew the attention of the Council to the matter but nothing had been done. It, was a death trap and two motor cycles had been over the embankment and other accidents.lf they had been fatal, more would have been heard about them. The Chairman said they were not responsible.

Mr Holt : T know that. Mr Mort said he mentioned the matter to the County Surveyor a month ago find he expected it would Le con- sidered in the near future. Mr Holt moved and Mr Davies Eeconded that the clerk be instructed to bring the matter before the notice of the County Council.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCII.—The anime. congregational tea and concert took place on New Year's Day. This annual function is always looked forward to with great pleasure, and a good attendeuec is therefore assured. A large number sat down to tea in the afternoon, and a still larger audience was present for the concert in the evening. Mr Thomas Critchley (church treasurer) presided.—The Chairruan, after wish- ing all a very Happy New Year, said he had a very healthy report to offer them us to the financial side of the Church's work. He bad received from various sections of tbe Church through Mr Howard (secretary) the substantial amount of £273 is. This amount had befit well invested, and had earned for them .£8 Is 4d in interest—(applause)—tbus making a total of .£287 2s 4d. In addition to this amount there was something like .£5 to crAne in from the ladies, making a grand total of £292 2s 4d. (Applause) He (the Chairman) would like to thank all those who had worked so hard in getting together such a noble sum for the Builaing Fund. He therefore thanked in the name of all present the ladies, the singers, and those who had in a special way helped to get together this sum of money. (Applause). A very fine concert progiamme had been arranged The following artistes took part and were loudly applauded for the efficient manner in which they rendered their respective parts:—Pianoforte Solo by Master Cyril Clayton was well received as also were the various effective renderings given by the Quartett Party; Miss Dorothy Holt a recitation entitled "Those five Little Brothers "; Miss Mary Gough a recitation entitled "MyS hadow; Mr J Clayton a song' Nuvarne"; Mrs Warbuton gave two songs entitled ; "My mother bids me bind my hair " and " When the flowing tide comes in"; Mr T Leah created much merriment with his humorous readings ; Mr Warburton gave two songs "Trooper John Ludlow " and "The Old Black Mare,'; Miss Whitmore's two items were songs entitled " On the Banks of Allan Water" and "My Dear Soul "; Miss Bertha Daniels a recitation entitled "Not our Time "; two dialogues given by the Misses Elsie Holt, Lydia Foden, Bertha Daniels and Messrs Reggie Clayton,and Albert Foden were well received;the Misses Shakeshaft a pianoforte duet; Master Fred Soarisbrick

NEW YEAR'S PARTY.—The annual New Year's party was held in the Sunday school on New Year's day. CAROL SERVICE —As is usual on the first Sunday after Christmas, instead of having the usual evensong and sermon at Orford Church, the service took the form of a carol service without any sermon. WESLEYAN CHuneit.—The annual watch night social in connection with the above church was held in the Wesleyan Sunday schools on New Y( ar's Eve. DEAT'fi OF A LICENSEE.—The death occurred suddenly on Saturday morning of Mr ;Joseph Davies, licensee of the Sportsman lull. Mr Davies got up about 7 a.m. and from all appearance was in his usual health. A short time after he had occasion to go into the garden and not returning for some time his sister becama anxious and asked her brother-in-law, Mr Dwerryhouse, if he would see where he was. He found the deceased with his head and arms in the water tub, which was a:low one. It was suggested that he had an attack of heart failure and fell against the tub. Medical aid was at once summon ed and on arrival Dr Murray pronounced life extinct.— The " Sportsman" Inn was previously kept by Mr Davies s mother, and on her death between two and three years ago he tecathe the lice, see. The inn has been iu the family's name over (10 years. Mr Davies had in earlier years taken a good deal of interest in the welfare of the village.—An inquest was held on Tues- day at deceased's residence by the Deputy Coroner (Mr F A Jones) Dr Murray, said he attribtited death to heart failure, tha jury afterwards returning a verdict of " Death from natural causes."—The funeral took place on Wednesday at Great Sankey. Much sympathy is fort by the villagers for deceased's relatives their sad bereavement.

INDEPENDENT METHODIST CHURCH, FEARNHEAD.— The annual tea party and concert were heid on Christmas Night, Mr Wm Seed occupied the chair. Numerous recitations were nicely rendered by the children. Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission. There was a good attendance at the service held at H Wycliffe Cockhedge Mission on New Year's Eve. Mr 13 Farrar presided and in the course of his address, said , that it was owing to the service being the last of the kind that would be held in that building, be had been -1 i persuaded to conduct the same. It was his privilege to I attend the first service of the kind held at the mission. -i Much good work had been accomplished and they were looking forward to a bright and prosperous future. It was a time for seriousness, for stocktaking, for thank- fulness to God for His goodness and mercy of the past, a time for decision for the future. We knew not our time. Time and eternity were in God's hands. Let us decide to servo God, to trust Him, and even the darkest hour would be brightened. Miss Nellie Smith sang a solo. Mr J Garnet and Mr Fred Mortal! also took part • in the service. sqt-M Baptist Church Colborne-street. ,H There was is large congregatien at the watch-night , service at this church on Tuesday evening. The pastor ' (Rev II Ogden) took for the subject of his address the .1 words "Let me go for the day breaketh." The end of 1 the year, be fe.id, found vacant places in many homes, hut no one was indispensable to the purposes of God. , • • If the worker died the work must go onond so the close ; of the year taught them that God crowned His mercies by removing the old and supplementing it with . the new. They learnt in a multitude of ways that here they had no abiding city, and that God moved one ' generation to male . way for the next one. In the removal of men and women, no matter how great and good they might have been,there vas the mercy of God. If they could take out the personal element of loss they would he able to see more clearly that there was a ' Divine Providence in death. If they had hope in God . it gave life a new meaning. All the prosaic days and , drab hours of monotonous tasks were suffused with. new light but for eternity, not for themselves, hut for . God. Who had a parsonal interest in their lives. . ' Bold-street Wesleyan Church. , The preacher at Bold-street on Sunday morning was': the Key II Williamson, 8.A., who chose for his subject , "The Great Commandment " (Matt. xxii. 37-39). It , is somewhat difficult for the English mind to grasp the ... , ill real significance of the word " Dive "as applied to 1 God. The Greeks had three words for the three distinct meanings which the word contains. But the ~., English mind drags in the ideas of the love of the sexes, , and the love of earthly ftiendship, as well as the idea . of the higher spiritual 13ve. The love of God is based , on veneration, worship, awe and esteem. It contains . two elf ments. First, the power of spiritual apprecia- tion of the truth, purity and worthiness of the., Almighty ; and secondly, the practical element which causes the lover to seek with heart laud soul to produce, , God's character in his own life. Whatever speaks to': his conscience becoaaes a law in his life; and be is com- pelled to study the spirit in which he lives, to see if it .1 conforms to the will of the All,Holy. The inclusion of . the love of man in the text is not an accidental, butt. logical connection. because the lover of God must strive to reproduce in mankind what he sees in God. = . The Church sees in man not only physical need and -1 happiness, but the great spiritual heritage. Social., 'al retorms to her are only a means to an end, viz., the salvation of the soul. And when we have solved all our social problems and brought Utopia to our England,the duty will still remain to her—to make man in tact what ~ ha is in ideal, a sou of God. Willi

Wycliffe Congregational Church. The Rev J C Onnerod, M.A., of Airdrie, preached morning and evening in Wycliffe Congregational Church on bucciay. Addressing the children at the morning service he told an allegorical story of a journey from the City of Selfishness to the City of God, showing the difficulties in the path of life and the reward to those who overcome them. His sermon had reference to the significance of Christmas and its teaching, being based on the text" Lo, the star which they saw in the East, went before them till it came and stood over where the young child was " (Matt. ii. 9). He pointed out that whilst the astrologers who followed the star were regarded as the star-gazers and visionaries of their time they divined the significance of the importance to the world of the birth of Christ whilst the political wise-acres of the day saw nothing at all. Not only were these wise men of the East star- gazers but they had the moral courage to come down from the high watch-towers to make known the birth of the Prince of Peace. Referring to the Peace Con- ference now sitting in London, the preacher ssid it was an auspicious season for negotiations for peace and he hoped the spirit of the season would work its magic powers on the hearts of the delegates towards peace. Taking for his text at the evening service the words " They crucified Him "(March xv- 25v.) the preacher said it was now admitted that the crucifixion of Christ was the greatest mistake in history. It was the work of the Scribes, Pharisees and Sadducees, who rejected Christ. The people who crucified Him were not bad people; on the other hand they cared for goodness,loved charity and worshipped God. The preacher proceeded to contrast these opponents of Christ with these who accepted Him—the disciples, publicans and sinners. The latter knew their short- comings and confessed them, the former knew their virtues and in a self-complacent mood gloried in them. It was not the world, the flesh and the devil which crucified Christ, but self-glorifying and self-complacent people who shut their e 3 es to goodness in others. The preacher warned his hearers against the sin of self- satisfaction and self-congratulation. It prevailed in the churches and amongst professedly religious people just as in the da3s of the Pharisees. They were not to think of other people's sins but of their own and have a. sense of their need for forgiveness before God.

CHEMICAL MANUFACTURERS' SECRETS. TWO EMPLOYEES FINED AT NORTHWICH. Two former employees of Brunner, Mond and Co., chemical manufacturers, named James Marsh, of Moreton-street, Winnington, and Robert Yarwood, assistant timekeeper at the company's Lostock works, were charged at Northwich on Saturday with stealing and receiving 'certain secret papers and documents belonging tothe company between January, 1909, and April, 1911. Mr. Bodkin, who prosecuted, said that Yarwood had been employed by the company three years, and was engaged in a part of the premises were highly important documents were kept containing records of processes and experiments. The allegation was that be took some of them and passed them on to Marsh, who had for years been collecting secret information in regard to the works. Mr Bsncroft, of Northwich, on behalf of Yarwood, said he was a scholarship boy who had shown great ability at school. He urged that there was no felonious intent in taking the papers from the company's posses- sion and giving them to Marsh. He had not received a copper for doing so. The Bench tined Yarwood .£5 or a month's imprison- ment. Marsh, against whom a further charge was pre- ferred of corruptly offering consideration to John Daffern for procuring information, was fined £25 or three months' imprisonment.

PADGATE.

WATCH-NIGHT SERVICE.—The usual watch-night service was held in the IS'esleyan Church. A short excellent address was given by the Rev E Morgan and us usual the congregation dispersed amidst New Year greetings and wishes. PAROCHIAL TEA.—The annual Sunday School tea meeting and entertainuient in conne.ction with Christ Church took place on Thursday evening. About 130 persons sat down to a substantial repast prepared under the capable superintendence of Mrs Grounds. A grand miscellaneous concert was given by well-known local artistes, whose reputations, as a rule, guarantee an excellent programme, and Thursday's entertainment was no exception. Those taking part were: Madame Sara Silcock, Mr John McArthur, Mr A Dutton, Mr J H Draper. The patter and concertina work of Mr Sam Aukland were greatly appreciated. A dramatic sketch " Henpecked" presented by Messrs W Critchley and F Povey and the Misses Horridge,Rathbone and Critchley was very much enjoyed by those assembled. The Vicar in a short address gave a resume of parish work during the past year and voiced the thanks of the audience to the artistes. Mr Blackwell acted us accompanist and afterwards played for dancing which brought to a close at midnight a very enjoyable even- ing. Mr Critchley was M.C.

===== 0002601_19130104_art0139.txt | included=true | included =====
The Religious Progress of the Year. Rev W Bracecamp and the Love of Pleasure.

In the course of a review of thel year at St Paul's Church on Sunday morning the Vicar (the Rev W Bracecamp) said the political events of the year had been important and fraught with great power to mould their country's future. Opinions differed, but they might all agree that they were in the hands of Him Who was "King of kings and Lords of lords "—the only ruler of princes and governor among the nations. They might all unite to pray, that He would so direct and prosper things that they •might result in "the advancement of His glory, the good of His church, and the safety, honour and welfare of our Sovereign and his dominions." The commercial results of the year on the whole had been most successful. There remained the religious history of the year, not less important than the others—hut less generally and carefully studied —the religious history of their country, church and themselves. They saw people still too much devoted to pleasure and frivolity, and per- meated with the spirit of worldliness. They saw a certain section of society too much estranged and alienated from religion and religious bodies. On the one hand the powers of unbelief and the apostles of infidelity were preying upon society and making considerable headway. On the other hand there were various religious bodies, with new vigour in their respective spheres, winning souls for God. Their own Church, the Church of England,national and established, not because the State had erected, organized, endowed or paid her, had established herself in far-off times, when the State itself was yet disorganized and with- out settled government. She had grown with the nation's growth, permeated the nation's life, stood for the nation's rights,and had gathered power and acquired property from many sources. Thei r Church had continued to offer her ministrations to all Billie and had endeavoured to bring the Gospel home to high and low, rich and poor, and if they looked from top to bottom of the social scale they would all acknowledge that she had met with a success that must encourage to new and greater efforts. She bad shown increased vitality and zeal and a growing disposition to adapt herself to the masses. There had been growing up an earnest desire for salutory reforms, but they had been in a measure hindered and marred by the strife and division which too often accompanied reviving activity and earnestness, when men's minds were in a ferment about the most important 'problems of life, and by want of agreenrent as to where they should alter and how they should reform. They noted signs of renewed activity on the part of those who agitated for the severance of Church and State, and who must be regarded as the enemies of the National Church, since they sought to cripple her resources, impair her usefulness and diminish her influence. They were enemies, too, of the State's best interests, for they sought to take from her the guarantee of religion and to force upon her the practical denial of God The moral of all this to clergy and laity alike was the necessity of union, of tolerance and forbearance, of a readiness to sink differences and devote themselves to the work of building up believers in their most holy faith and winning the unconverted to the Gospel of Christ.

48 Years In the Ministry.

Manchester is to lose one of its best-known preachers by the resignation of the Rev Dr Gfrodrich, of Chorlton-road Congregational Cuurch. For forty-eight yeers Dr Goodrich has laboured in the ministry, and twenty-two years of that time he has spent in Manchester. He preached his farewell sermon on Sunday. Dr Goodrich is shortly to take up his residence et Colwyn Bay, and he will be succeeded by the Rev F Y Leggatt. St. John's Presbyterian Church. A watch-night service was held at this church on Tuesday evening. The Rev W Reid gave an impres,ive addrets in which he said the philosophy of life was to be found in our ability to recognize the problems which settled as a cloud upon our pathway. Appropriate hymns were sung, including "When Israel, of the Lord beloved," written by Sir Walter Scott ; and at the close of the service the pastor wished the members of the congregation" A happy and prosperous New Year."

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. An open meeting was held on Sunday, presided over by Mrs. Cummings, of New Zealand, who spoke of the pleasure and joy of meeting the members. Although her home ecross the seas was so far away, the oneness of our Father God and our sonship in Him, made our meeting with God's people sweet and helpful.—Mrs. Wright, of New Zealand, gave the address. She said her mind for a week or two had been engeged in wondering what message she should bring the members. God had led her to bring the messoce " God loved, therefore God gave." Christmas time filled our minds with presentations or gifts. We could trace back the giving to the Wise men from the East, who brought their gifts to present to Jesus. God's greatest gift was love, embodied in the birth of Jesus. Love moved the world; it saved and lifted the fallen. The speaker gave some striking illustrations of God's doings with the outcasts. He gave to the world its Redeemer and Saviour. Might we give God our best service, our love and helpfulness to our own and to all around us. She spoke of our powers of helpfulness in showing our love and sympathy both at home and abroad.—Miss Alice lieswick read the lesson. Mrs. Wright led the pra3er.—The President thanked the ladies for their service and message.—The addresses were greatly enjoyed.

the lines " Hark how all the welkin rings glory to the King of Kings ' were described as a "dreadful variant." But it must be remembered that this was the original form of the hymn as Charles Wesley wrote it in 1730, and it has the merit of being true to the Scripture narrative. The form now in common use was an alteration made in 1760 probabiy by Martin MAdati, and it contains two "inexactitudes." For it was not the herald angel (there was but one) who si:ag or "said " it was a multitude of the heavenly host attending upon the herald angel, and their theme was not " Glory to the new-born King " ; it was what Charles Wesley's original second line describes. The English Hymnal has done well in giving the two versions of the hymn

side by side. As to the tune, it is probably as successful as most adaptations are, though anyone who tries it over to its proper words (" Let our theme of praise ascending" in the English rendering of the Festgesang)will see how it loses by the change. Meanwhile, though it has succeeded in displacing older and perhaps feebler tunes, some of which I have in manuscript, yet it has one great defect. The criginal hymn was in four-line stanzas. These have been replaced by eight-line stanzas with refrain. Now if there is a refrain, the music should rise to it as a climax. In the present case, the music of the refrain is a mere repetition of the two preceding lines, suitable ecough in Mendelssohn's original setting where the words are repeated also. but unsuitable here as giving the impression not of a climax but of an after-thought.— Yours &c. Walton Vicarage

L. J. T. DAR WALL,

ROMANISM V. PROTESTANTISM. Sir,—Mr. Richatdson, in replying to me, quotes me as astonished. I may say it is not astonishment but disgust that I feel. My entry into this correspondence is not to impart any political aninans, but simply to reply to attacks on the aspect of the Christian religion from my point of view, in pressing my views, I trust that from both sides, there will be a complete truthful- ness, without any suppression of fact or misleading statement. How far we may succeed in carrying this out must he left to the .indoment of you and your readers. I trust that he will bear in mind that Phil Squcd is equally as honourable as himself. What we, as Protestors hold, is that the dogmas and articles of faith, at present taught by the Roman Church, are not the original articles of the Christian faith taught by Christ and His Apostles. We main- tain t4t the primitive Church, in the course of succeeding ages, became contaminated by the self- seeking interests of men of the woild. - that when Rome ceased to be the capita of the civil world, she aspired to graft her pagan worship on the Christian religion, and become the capitAl of the world's religion —that down the pages of time Ri me has continually been adding new dogmas acid articles to her doctrines —that fina ly, the Church which, according to Mr Richardson never chano.es,h,s r ever ceased to change. After the days of barbarism, when men began to settle down to learning and civilization. Rome became the opponent of all progress in the liberal arts, and we find her drifting far frcm the one true God, into a sort of polytheistical republic, where the whole host of heaven is worshipped and, the Virgin Mary seems to be placed higher, aid held in more honour, than the Blessed Trinity. In England the first dawn of light came in 1380 when Wycliffe published his translation of the Bible, and from that day this dear land of ours has shone on brighter and brighter unto the perfect day in circulat- ing the Scriptures. From that day Britain has never wanted for men to stand and endure 1, r the Gospel's sake. But the era proper of the Reformation extends from 15I6,when Erasmus published his New Tetament in England, to 1688, when the ccatsiastical and political constitution of our country was settled on a Protestant basis under William of Orange. "The period embraced by these two dates is the most glorious in our annals. It was fruitful throughout in men of great character, and in events of world-wide influence. More especially was it adorned towards the middle by a constellation of elegant scholars, accom- plished theologians, of great statesmen and holy martyrs, who have never perhaps been surpassed in any age, whether of our own or of any other nation. Nor did the Reformation develop itself as a purely spiritual existEnce. It made society the companion of its progress and the partner of its triumphs. The divine principles at its centre sent the pulses of a new life through the body corporate, and thus it gave us a new State as well as a renovated Church. Foster- ing every liberal study, and encouraging every generous art, it built up around itself a bulwark of social enlightenment and political freed, in " A work like this was dearly purchased, and should not be lightly thrown away; it cost the toil, the blood, and the tears, of two whole centuries. Mr. Richardson, like most Romanists, delights to date the Reformation from the squabble between the Pope and Henry VIII. over the latter's harem. What- ever Mr Richardson may think of Henry VIII. the Papal Lagate attended his court and, for some considerable time, was Lord High Chancellor if England. It was during the preceding reign of Henry VII. that the then Papal Legate (Cardinal Morton) reported to Rome the dreadful demoralized state of the monasteries in England. Innocent VIII, the then Pope, issued a call to Cardinal Morton to abate the scandal ' • but, inveterate abuses take long to eradicate, and Cardinal Morton, Henry VII. and Innocent VIII. were in their graves before anything could be done. We find Cardinal Wolsey upon the scene as Papal Lagate at the Court of Henry VIII. Wolsey found some of the Monasteries in disorder, financially and morally, aud immediately applied to Pope Clement VII. for powers to suppress them. Clement complied with his request, and issued his bill or rescript to the Cardinal Legate, authorizing him, as he saw fit, to suppress all and every monastery in the whole realm of England. So if this was a measure of robbery, sacrilege and spoliation, it was done at the bidding of Rome and by its servants. Let me conclude by drawing Mr Richardson's atten- tion to the following extract from the late Dr. New- man's lectures on Rornanistn and popular Pro- testantism. He said of the Church of Rome :—" In truth she is a Church beside herself, abounding in noble gifts and rightful titles, but unable to use them religiously ; crafty,obstinate, wilful, malicious, cruel, unnatural, as madmen are. Or, rather she may be said to resemble a demoniac, possessed with principles, thoughts, and tendencies not her own. . Thus she is her real self only in name, and till God vouchsafe to restore her, we must treat her as if she were that evil one which governs her." What Pro- testant can make a fiercer attack on Romanism than the above from a man who became a Cardinal of Rome? I am &c.

PHIL SQUOD,

FARMER'S CLUBS IN ONTARIO.

Since the i-na-u-g-t;r;ti-o-n-Wil-he-sy-s-t-e.ri--.7,1-a.ppointing district representatives of the Department of Agricul- ture in each county in Ontario, there has sprung into prominence the farmers' clubs. They are fostered by these young agriculturists in co-operation with some of the more advanced farmers. The entire idea is for the spread of general agricultural information, and the introduction of new ideas and new methods. These clubs, though not very large, give the farmers in any one section the opportunity of getting together and talking over in a business-like manner anything which appeals to them as being of interest and value to the community. Occasionally outside speakers are brought in to address the club on scientific work, but more often they arrange the addresses among themselves ; thus they are of a double value. Young men are often among the speakers. It is an opportunity for every man if he wishes to take advantage of it to become proficient on the public platform. It must not be supposed that these clubs are for agricultural matters solely; they also take charge in many cases of the social work in the commLnity. Probably they will be of more value to the Ontario farmer in this say than in many others. For, where there is no organisation to arrange social affairs, this side of the farmer's life is frequently neglected. People coming from Great Britain, who carry with them memories of the little village and the merry times experienced will find that these clubs in a greater or less degree reproduce their former conditions. It is the opportunity for every newcomer to become better acquainted with the methods of agriculture and also to become acquainted with the farmers in his particular neighbourhood aud some of the more pro- minent farmer's from all over the province. The club is not managed with the idea of being the means of making money; consequently, any fees which are charged are only nominal. Anyone, no matter how poor, can join. The district schoolhouse, or local hall, is generally the place of meeting. Recently organised, these clubs are re-organising in no small way the social and agricultural life of Ontario. It is the personal contact with men who have become successful that does most to encourage the "indifferent farmer."

The Examiner, Saturday, Januar:. 4, 1913.

On New Year's Eve, Mr and Mrs Woolley, sen entertained about SD guests in the Buckley-street Schoolroom, the occasi.in being their silver wedding. They were the recipients of many c npratularicns during the day. Mr and Mrs Woolley were married at the Wycliff.A Church in 1897, by the Rev John Yonge. Mr W Flewitt of Manchester, proposed the healthlof the host and hostess,and they were spoken of in complimentary terms by Mr James lirtnston and Mr Edward Plinston (old associates f the old Dallam Lane Chapel). During the evening, Mr Thomas Hewitt (president of the church) presided over an after meeting, when advantage was taken of the happy event to present Mr and Mrs Woolley with a token of esteem for the valu.ble work they are always ready to do in connection with the Methodist Free Church. Mr and Mrs Woolley were referred to in complimentary terms by Mr A Taylor (superin- tendent of the School), and Mr J J FairlatiSt. The chairman afterwards called upon the oldest member of the church, Mr J Drury, to make the presentation.

He banded Mr Woolley a handsome silver tea service inscribed—" Buckle -street Methodist Free Church and Sunday Scnool Warrington, presented to Mr and Mrs Woolley on the occasion of their Silver Wedding, December 31st, 1912." On rising t;) respond, Mr Woolley was re:Tived with great applause. He thanked all the subscribers for the handsome gift he had received from them, and said he should treasure the beautiful present for the good feeling which had prempred its gift. Mr Woolley was a scholar in the old Dalt= Lane Chapel in 1871, and has been oh- sely connected with the Church all his life. He is the Church members' class leader a posiiion which he has held for about 10 years. He is also a Sunday School teacher, a deacon, and an enthusiastic worker in all branches of the Church._

Mr and Airs xVoolley were also the recipient of a beautiful bound book from the members of the Welsh Church in Crosfield-street.

University. His humorous and dramatic recitals were much appreciated. During an interval Mr W Silcock, the superinten- dent of the Sunday school, made a little speech, in the course of which he reported the satisfactory progress of the school, drawing special attention to the success of the newly-fot rued prftnary section. On Thursday the scholars' tea party was held. After tea a short programme was opened by Mr Alfred Dutton %%ith a humorous sketch entitled "The 11.69 express." Mr Dutton kept the youngsters laughing from start to finish and they insisted on an ei,core. Several scholars gave songs and recitations and the " stars in this ditection NA ere undoubtedly the little tots of the Primary Department. Miss Dring at the conclusion cf the songs, distributed the attendance prizes to the boys and girls, and the child:eb gave her a heat ty clap for her kindness. The remaindet of the evening was spent in the games and romps which the youngsters so much enjoy. The two tea meetings were the most successful of recent years and much praise is due to the ladies who worked so hard to bring this about.

'MESS HOURS 9 to 8 DAILY

THE INFIRMARY BALL.

Patrons of the annual ball in aid of the funds of the Warrington Infirmary and Dispensary are reminded that the function will be held in the Parr Ball, on Thursday next, January 9th. It is interesting to note that the first ball in aid of the funds of the Dispensary was held in the Town Hall, Warrington, on Thursday, November 25th, 1819. The ball, except on occasions of National misfortune, has been an annual event ever since that date. The com- mittee who arranged for the first ball were :—Mrs Blackburne, lady patroness ; Mr Thomas Lyon and Mr Glazebrook, managers. The stewards were :—The officers of the Chatity, Messrs Nicholson, Parr, Pemberton, Dakin, Greptiall, Bower, Haddcck, Stubbs Brandwood, Wagstaff, Pieta') ere and Lfre. The Ladies' Infirmary Ball Committee, with Messrs G Ireland Blackburne and J E Geenall as joint. hon. secretaries, have charge of the arrangements for the forthcoming ball, and they are making every provision for the comfort and entertainment of the guests. THE NEW YEAR.

How it was Welcomed at Warrington.

The New Year was not heralded in Warrington in the boisterous fashion characteristic of those towns where large communities of Scots fl-mot their own cherished tlogmanv rites before the wondering and nu-demonstrative Sassenach. The rinping of church bells, the sounding of works syrens, and the exchange of the traditional good wishes proclaimed the passing of 1912 and the dawn of 1913. When the town clock struck the hour of twelve a large crowd assembled at Market Gate joined hands and sang" Auld iang synti " with some approach to enthusiasm. In several places of worship watchnight services were held and large congregations took part in this impressive observat.ce of the passing of another of life's milestones. At St Paul's Church a somewhat novel feature was intro- duced, the bugle band of the Church Lads' Brigade scuncling the " Last Post" in the church yard as the last few moments of the old year ebbed out. Then with the advent of 1913, the bells rang out a welecminsz peal. New Year's Day was practically a gen,ral holiday and a large cro‘%d iesorted to the football match at Wilderepool. In the evening the many places of amusement had crowded "houses." GOLBORNE-STREET BAPTISTS.

The annual party in connection with Colborne- street Baptist Church was held on New Year's Day. About 100 members of the church and congregAtion sat down to a substantial tea. The arrangements had been made by the officers of the church, assisted by members of the Youog Men's Institute, no lady taking part in the preparations. Spec al praise is due to Mr B Few, Mr H Buckby, Mr JII Pepper, and Mr Fl Hall for the completeness of the arrangements, such being the excellence of the fare that the tea was voted one of the best that has been held in recent years. After tea there was a capital entertainment ntesided over by the pastor, the Rev H Ogden. In the course of a short address be referred to the ni.ity which existed in the church and the loyal plainer in which the people had supported him during the past 12 months. He made a special appeal for greater interest +o he taken in the work of the Sunday School, stating that attention devoted to the young was attention focussed on the right end of life.-- Mr E. Wynne Jones pre- sented the financial statement which showed a satis- factory state of affairs In thanking the friends for their help during the past sear he stated that they had done touch useful work and he asked that they would support the church even better in the future. The following artistes took part in a capital programme of songs and sketches : Messrs. 11 Battles, E A Sanders, and B Few, Mi,ses Tanner and Ada Earner. BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN TEA PARTY.

The congregational tea party in connection with Bold-street Wesleyan Sunday school was held on New Year's Day and was very well attended. The tables were presided over by the ladies of the Sunday tchool, and the catering arrangem.nts were carried out Miss Mills, assisted by several of the teachers. Shortly after 7 o'clock the cor.eert commenced and was presided over by the Rev H Williamson. Miss Clarice Newall delighted her hearers with her piano- forte solos widen were vety skilfully and tastefully played, and the audience were very pleased whed she responded to an encore. The vocalist was Miss Carrie Hastings, who is always a favourite. Most of the programme was taken up by Mr Charles Powell, Pro- fessor of Elocution and teacher at the Manchester

THE .LITE REV. J. S. HUGHES. FUNERAL AT HILL CLIFFE.

In the peaceful graxeyat ri attached to the old chapel at Hill (life, which he loved so well, the remains of the late Rev. J. 'S. Hughes were laid to rest on Saturday afternoon. There was a large gather- ing of relatives, friends and representatives of the Baptist Churches in Warrington and district. An impressive service was conducted in the chapel by the Rev Professor Ellis, of the Baptist College Marchester, and the Rev George Curtis, pastor at Hill Cliffe In the course of an address Prof. Ellis referred to the valuable work uone by Mr Hughes for the Baptist College as secretary for the eleven years previous to his removal to Coventry. Two churches had been served by him as pastor Irwell Terrace, rcup and Hill CHTe, before he took up work at Coventry (Webster-street). At both Bacup and Hill Giffe he lab.erred for frutteert years. Between the two pastorates he tested for two years, owing to a nervous breakdown. Friends from Bacup were present at the funeral to testify their esteem for their former pastor Many from Hill (life were there to acknow- ledge the mercies received during his ministry. Prof. Ellis impressed on the gathering the duty of being grateful for mercies even when withdrawn, and the value of tribulation in the refining of their characters, and the promise of victory through faith in Christ. The mourners were Mrs J S Hughes (widow), Mr H V Hughes (nephew), Mr Griffiths (brother-in-law), Mrs Griffiths and Mrs Jenkins (sisters), Mr William Arnold (brother-in-law), Mrs Arnold, Mrs Astbury (sisters-in-law), Mr Philips (brother-in-law), Mrs Philips (sister-in-law), Mrs Humphries and the Misses Griffiths (neices), the Rev E Dlvies, Mr E Davies (o,oventry), Mr R Ormiston (Winwick), Mr E England, Mr A J Willett, Mr F Bate. Mr B Griffiths, Mr Joe Moss. Mr and Mrs J \foss, Mr W Moss, Mr G Moss, Mr W Wilde, Mrs Whitfield, Mr Whitmore, Mrs Dodd, Mrs Cartwright, Mr H Edwards and a large number of tire members of the Hill (life congregation. Floral tributes were sent by " His sorrowing wife," " Evelyn and Kenneth," " Wilson and Annie and little Barbara," officers arid members of the Webster- street Baptist Church, Coventry; the Rev W and Mrs Arnold, Golborne ; "the MOSS family," and Mr and Mrs G Whitfield. Mr Stephens, of Warrington, carried out the funGral arrangements.

To the President of the Warrington Tariff Reform League.

Dear Mr. Alderman Bennett,—l have always had an appreciation of the point of view from which you approach the question of Tariff Reform. I take it to be that it is a means to an end, this end being Imperial unity. Hence lam moved to convey to you some reflections on the present position and trust you will not resent my writing publicly, as the subject is one of national and imperial , interest. I have no wish to intrude in the present difficulty in which the party to which you have allied yourself are placed. This I leave to be settled among themselves. What I desire to point out is that such difficulties are bound to emerge in a party composed of sections holding different and opposing ideals. Your own position has always been that 'of Mr. Chamberlain when he launched his proposals, proposals which have made politics so vital during the last ten years. You will remember in the life of the Duke of Devon- shire published in 1911, we have a letter from Mr. Chamberiain to the Duke in which he says : " But you refused to look at my proposals for Preference, which are put forward solely with the object of ensuring Imperial unity, and which under no possible circumstances would lead to any substantial, or indeed perceptible, protection to a home industry, it is ridiculous to suppose that 2s a quarter On corn would restore prosperity to agricuitut e, although th,-, farmers might possibly support it as a drowning man would catch at a straw. For my part I care only for the great question of Imperial unity, everything else is secondary, or consequential.'

You will further -remember that Mr. Balfour in one of his thoughtful speeches said :

"I have deprecated to-night the introduction of a Free Trade and Protectionists controversy into the quite alien subject of Fiscal Reform ; I have explained to the bet of my ability the way in which I would proceed with regard to retaliation. I have new told sou what I think about the most important of all these subjects, that, namely, of closer commercial union with our Colonies; and the reasJn that I made these earlier reservations was because I, for my part, believe that nothing will endanger the le.merial idea more than any rash introduction into it of the insular idea. Protection is insular or it is nothing ; it is alien therefore in its very essence and spirit—l do not say inconsistent, but alien in its very essence and spirit—to the Imperial idea, and the Imperial idea is the me which most appeals to me. " I find in the above quotations the reason (which would otherwise be to me a mystery) why you have allied yourself to and become the local head of the Fiscal Reform Move- ment. I wish, however, to point out that the Tariff Reform agitation has drifted entirely away from the conception of its founders. What Mr Balfour deprecated and feared has happened. Protection has shouldered aside the primary question of Imperial unity. Con- flicting ideas alien to the primary one have become dominant and this has produced all the difficulties which have beset the Tariff Reform Movement. The imperial conception, even when put forward by men of magnetic personality like Messrs. Balfour and Chamberlain, was not of itself sufficient to fire the enthusiasm of the electors and the movement soon degenerated into an appeal of sectional groups, buying the votes of one district by selling the industry of another, a characteristic of all Protectionist move- ments. In the country districts food taxes were exploited,in the manufacturing districts taxes on foreign goods were to keep out com- petition, at the same time raise a revenue, and food was to be no dearer, &c., &c. These separate appeals to opposing interests have failed, as they ought to have failed, in evolv- ing a workable scheme cemented together by an Imperial sentiment. Agriculturists who were looking for the benefits of Protection had their enthusiasm damped as in their morning paper they read speeches of leafers who had told town audiences that food would if anything, be cheaper. Manufacturers were puzzled to know whether their raw material would be considered raw enough to escape taxation, and their manufactured article finished enough to be protected, while the consumers as a whole did not rise to the idea of the empire being cemented altogether at their expense. The result has been weakness and discord. Tariff Reformers have succeeded in infusing a separate spirit of hostility into each of the sections whose support they have endeavoured to secure. You will remember a compromise, which agrarian Tariff Reformers hailed with satis- faction, was attempted bet ween Protec- tionists and Imperialists. It was proposed to put a duty, but a smaller one, on corn from the Colonies. This was ruled out by the leaders. Now we have the difficulty in regard to food taxes arising from another section—the consumers. Your own good sense must make it clear to you that no effective Preference can be given to the Colonies unless you tax food, and no confer- ence with the Colonies, as proposed by Mr. Bonar Law, can shake the truth of Mr. Chamberlain's statement, so honestly made in the early days of this controversy., that you cannot have effective Colonial Preference without food taxation, for the simple reason that the great bulk of Colonial imports are food stuffs. Here you have another indication how slack a bold Imperial unity has upon the electors, and if you want more proof read the ponderous Volume No. 3 con- taining the evidence before the Agricultural Committee of the Tariff Reform League. Locally you will agree that (apart from your own expressed aspiration for Imperial unity through Tariff Reform) your local Association and the kindred Women's Association are practically silent in both press and platform on this aspect and all the advocacy is for what Mr Balfour rightly con- siders an alien and insular question—that of Protection pure and simple, a Protection similar to that adopted by Germany, Austria and France and repudiated by both Messrs Chamberlain and Balfour at the launching of the Imperial Preference Project. I desire to impress upon you that the idea of Imperial Unity is not confined to one party. There are many Liberals who are ready to respond to the" Call of the Blood," who do not wish to shirk the "great and grateful burden of Empire." They are with you in the thought. Whom should I trust if not my kin? And whom Account so near in natural bonds as these, Boin of my mother England's mighty womb, Nursed on my Mother England's mighty knees, And lull'd as r was lull'd in glory and in gloom With cradle song of her protecting seas ? But after years of thought we have become more and more convinced that the unity we both value cannot be attained on the basis of the cash nexus, or by a system of fiscal arrangement which, for the sake of a few favoured industries in the Colonies and Mother country, would injure the whole home population. We consider the cement of Preference attained by Protection instead of holding the Empire together is likely to change to dynamite, liable at any time violently to strain and may log to break up the Empire. CHARLES E. PARKER. Penketh Lodge, January Ist, 1913.

===== 0002601_19130104_art0150.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEBACLE OF THE FOOD TAXERS,

By A. H. CrosHeld,

Since Humpty Dumpty had his famous tumble there has been nothing like it—no such spectacle of hopeless, bewildered political floundering. It reminds me of a beetle, or a sheep on its back kicking out— aimlessly; or of a horse that has been too venturesome on a slippery pavement and has come a cropper. You see the unfortunate animal lying on the ground between twisted and broken shalts, and hampered by the confused wreck of the harness—an entangle- ment only made the worse by its struggles. That is the plight in which the Food Taxers have landed themselves, and one turns to see what their scribes in the Press, and their leaders on the platform have to say about it. Let us first consult Mr. Garvin, the prolific, energetic, versatile Editor of the London "Observer." For quite a long time now it has fallen to Mr. Garvin to provide the Tory Party periodically with a policy: or at any rate he has assumed the role of leader in chief of the Tory Party in general and the more extreme group of the " Die-Hards " and Food Taxers in particular. Having more than once boxed the compass with amazing ' agility (there has been a very notable example during the Home Rule controversy) Mr. Garvin has time and again led his Party and his followers a pretty dance; but on one question he has shown unswerving consist- ency. Mr. Garvin is nothing if he is not a "Whole Hogger," " Confederate " and Food Taxer, and therefore when like the " Daily Mail," the " Times" went wrong and funked food taxes, Mr. Garvin vowed vengeance on Lord Northcliffe, and only ten days ago warned his party of the "fierce and unspar- ing controversy" in store for those Tories who failed to back up the Chamberlain policy. Especially was his indignation directed against the " Times " newspaper. Like the man in that once so popular play "Our Boys," it was a case of" Give up the ' Times ' and take the Telegraph." But now the " Telegraph " has gone wrong too! Yes,even Lord Burnham and the" Telegraph," once upon a time such devoted disciples of Mr. Chamberlain. And it is not only Tory dailies like the "Mail," the "Telegraph," and in the provinces, the " Liverpool Courier," the "Yorkshire Post," the "Nottingham Guardian" and so on, that have to be reckoned with ; the weeklies are now joining in the chorus' There is the "Saturday Review," telling Unionists they will be mad ! if they do not throw food taxes overboard, these food taxes which, as it points out with perfect truth, have " have scared large numbers of voters in Lancashire." And then there is the "Sunday Times," another well known Conservative journal writing as follows : • "At last the Conservative Party has the oppor tunity of freeing itself horn the handicap which assent to an economic heresy has imposA upon it for a number of years, and of becoming cnce again a powerful and dereimining force in the direction of the Nations destinies. According to a trustworthy estimate ba'f the Party are opposed to the most pragnant expres- sicn of this heresy the Food Taxes. But as the Patty machine is n the bands of the Protectimists the majority hare for a long time been unable to make their voices heard in an elf cive mariner."

_ What has Mr. Garvin to say to that? How does he like these remarks in his Sunday contemporary, professedly an organ of the same Party as the " Observer " ? Perhaps we shall hear next week. Meantime, last Sunday Mr. Garvin was for once in a way less cocksure and precise than he is wont to be when lecturing his party. At this crisis in their affairs he is—contrary to his custom—waiting for his leaders instead of showing them •the way; and so for the most part he contents himself with indulging in generalities. For instance, speaking of the Unionist party, he treats his readers to the sapient observation that "the one matter cf supreme impot tance to it is to get upon ground where it can stand ; where in the eyes of all men it evidently means to stand; where it can combat with all its might."_

But that is just what poor Humpty Dumpty cannot do. Does not Mr. Garvin even now perceive that he has not a leg to stand on ? "No sane manager," continues the Editor of the "Observer," "could think of merely dropping the food taxes in a manner that would split the party to the base at last ; deprive it of the Midlandi ; sweep it away in the Counties. Rural voters will never tolerate a tariff only on what they buy with none for what they produce." That last sentence deserves the special attention of people in manufacturing districts —and there are not a few of them—who have been deluding themselves with the idea that Food Taxes can be dropped and Tariff Reform limited to a duty on manufactured articles. Even if that were possible it would be the height of folly to cripple Lancashire trade by adding in a thousand and one ways to its cost of production. But as some 3f us have all along pointed out, it is not possible, and it is significant and useful to have this pointed reminder from this leader of the " Con- federates." I have occupied so much space in these references to the Tory Press that one or two remarks on the attitude of the "Confederate " and Tory leaders on the platform had better stand over till next week. January Ist, 1913.

A. H. CROSFIELD.

LEAGUE OF PITY.

FANCY DRESS BALL.

1 On Friday, last week, a fancy dress dance was held at the Winmarleigh Cafe in aid of the funds of the League of Pity, N.S.P.C.C., which is celebrating its Broadbent, who wahnonivbearssair)eye.n Itwa secretary of n since its foundation. There were about 78 present, and the evening began with games for the children, sarranged de b League .ea Miss followed by dancing. Miss Dorothy Browne, an honorary member of the League, won the prize for the best costume. She had a graceful Turkish dress of black and pink with a pearl headdress. Miss Lucy Skinner, an6ther member had an equally pretty Eastern dress of blue and gold and Mr Skinner was in Highland dress. Miss Gladys Waring had a very smart black carnival dress and wore powdered hair. Mrs Langdale and Mrs Roger Willcocks looked extremely well as Italian peasants, and Mr Frank Willcocks was in white ducks with a sun helmet. Amongst the cbildren, Miss Elfreda Bousfield, the y,.ungest present, looked the best as Mistress Mary, with silver bells and cockle shells, and Mr Efarry Dodd was in splendid form as a boy scout. Miss Leslie wore a pretty quaint dress with a mob cap. ,Miss Tinsley made an ideal Quakeress. Miss Sylvia Broadbent wore her grandmother's dress of blue brocade with hair dressed to the period and a high comb, and Miss L Wareing wore a beautifully embroidered kimono.

Dancing went on with great spirit till one o'clock and Lady Greenall having kindly given a donation towards the expenses, a sum of .£6 was cleared to help on the admirable work of N.S.P.C.C. Inspector Wainwright made himself responsible for taking the tickets at the door.

ARCNITECTS AND THE EDUCATION

COMMITTEE.

The Warrington Education Committee, like most other bodies of similar constitution (says the " Archi- tects' and Builders' Journal ") has no very clear understanding of its correct attitude towards archi- tects. T hat, at all events, is the chief impression left upon us after perusing a report of a recent debate by the committee on a motion to rescind a former resolu- tion, in which it had been decided to abandon the competition system, and to'appoint the architects of one school to design two other schools. The advocate of recession urged that the work ought tD be thrown tohpaetn3T pceormepeentitt.iowna;B baunt aads he eguaatpe appeared to think ion also for the selected architects, it is clear that the proposed reduc- tion on the recognised rate would create a rather worse grievance than that which the speaker was opposing. It may be remembered that in the. first instance the Education Committee declined ts ask the R.1.8.A. to appoint an assessor whereupon the Institute issued the customary notice requiring its members to abstain from competing. This prohibition had no deterrent effect on tho• committee, and in the upshot they had the choice of 37 designs, 73 members of the Institute having with- drawn. When, therefore, the eomtnittee afterwards found that the firm of architects they had secureci were serving them well, it was natural to consult the successful firm with respect to the additional schools. But the motives undellying this decision are such that architects should make common cause against their recurrence. In such cases it is not enough for the R.1.8.A. to intervene with a curt notification that the conditions are unsatisfactory, for in too many instances the mere advice to members, to abstain from competition has no other effect than that of self-denying ordinance on the part of those who. lovallyftbine by it, because the Institute is regarded— ignoradtly enough—by the average young burgess as. being more pretentious than powerful. Invest it with. such credentials as a Registration Act would providei, and the immeciaie gain in status would be followed by, an enormous accession of material force and moray influence. The power and authority of the Institute, and the consequent respect in which it would be held, would then place it on much the same efficient footing as the law and medical associations, which so completely control the professions they represent :thatt even Governments stand in wholesome awe of them,. while local authorities never dream of flouting, their dicta. Of course, the Warrington resolution was not rescinded, but if the Institute had been able t.o speak with more tan merely academic authority, we should, have been spared this and many another absurd, debate. Its advice would have been sought automatic- ally and accepted as a matter of course. At the meeting of the Town Council next Tuesday• Councillor Pemberton will again move the rescinding, of the Education Commit'ees resolution,

. . - . „.„ itcs. ~.• • _ QUERIES AND ANSWERS. OUR READERS are invited to Bend legal queries to be answered week by week. Inquiries must be short, concise, and plainly written, and the name and address of the writer given to show that the inquiry is genuine. The replies are by a member of the profession, and every effort is made to secure accuracy, but we cannot assume any responsibility for the opinion or advice given. Mark all envelopes enclosing queries " Legal," and address them ti the EDITOR " EXAMINER," WARRINGTON. If you do not wish initials to be used in the reply please suggest a ?tom-de-plume. INSURANCE.—On the 15th inst. NOV ICE.—In the County Court, Warrington. You Ehould certainly engage a solicitor as the claim is not easy to substantiate. RUGBY.—It seems to have been a pure accident: You have no case. MISTRESS.—(I) Not if you acted in good faith (2) one month's wages or one month's notice. C.H.—See answer to "Insurance "above. H.N.C.—Two independent -vitnesses. Both must see the Testator sign and must attest in his presence and in the presence of each other. DAVID.—(I) Both are liable, and you should join them in the action. (2) In the Warrington County Court. (3) Nursing, medical attendance, extra nourishment, pain and suffering. You will require the help of a solicitor. LEGAL.—(I) The order of the Court was binding even though your relative did not attend. (2 and 3) The loan holders and shareholders and the ordinary creditors should act together and attack the debentures. We should say s-,rneone is sure to act in the matter. If not you should consult a solicitor. (4) It all depends on circumstances. Surely the sanction of the Court was obtained? It seems to us that you should take advice without delay. Take all documents in your possession with you.
